# Item

## label
Bacterial membrane lacks sterols

## id
CON-INF-F8912B54FCA3A5

## canonical_key
bacteria.cytoplasmic-membrane.lacks-sterol-vs-eukaryotic

## definition
Unlike a eukaryotic cell membrane, the prokaryotic (**bacterial**) cytoplasmic membrane is a phospholipid bilayer studded with membrane proteins but __contains no sterols such as cholesterol__.

The **sole exception is Mycoplasma**, whose membrane does contain sterol.

## explicit_objective
State the feature that distinguishes the bacterial cytoplasmic membrane from a eukaryotic membrane, and name Mycoplasma as the one bacterial exception.

## concept_type
definition

under review

## subtopic

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## aliases
Bacterial membrane sterol content
Absence of sterol in bacterial cytoplasmic membrane
Prokaryotic vs eukaryotic membrane composition

## pitfalls
Assuming every prokaryotic membrane is completely sterol-free with no exception. Mycoplasma is the one bacterial genus whose membrane does contain cholesterol, scavenged from the host, which compensates for having no cell wall to give it rigidity.

# Item

## label
Bacterial ribosomes are 70S

## id
CON-INF-29351FD540E214

## canonical_key
bacteria.ribosome.70s-protein-synthesis

## definition
Prokaryotic bacterial cells use **70S ribosomes** (a **30S** and a **50S** subunit) as the __site of protein synthesis__, unlike the **80S** ribosomes of eukaryotic cytoplasm.

This size difference underlies the **selective toxicity** of several antibiotic classes — aminoglycosides, macrolides and tetracyclines — for bacteria over the human host.

## explicit_objective
State that bacterial ribosomes are 70S and are the site of protein synthesis, as the correct statement about prokaryotic cells among distractors describing eukaryotic features.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids

ASU-INF

## universities
asu

## learner_years
1

## blueprint_weight
0.25

## exam_weight_by_year
ASU_Y1=0.25

## clinical_relevance
0.35

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
General bacteriology

## subtopic
Bacterial cell envelope

## microtopic
Cytoplasmic membrane and ribosomes

## nanotopic

## aliases
70S ribosome
Bacterial protein synthesis site
30S and 50S ribosomal subunits

## pitfalls
Confusing a prokaryotic cell for a eukaryotic one and expecting a prominent nucleolus, histone-coiled DNA or rough endoplasmic reticulum — none of these exist in a bacterial cell. Only the 70S ribosome performing protein synthesis is genuinely correct among those distractors.

## article_ids
ART-INF-BACTERIAL-MEMBRANE-RIBOSOME

2-Concerning prokaryotic bacterial cells, which of the following is correct: a) It have prominent nucleolus b) 70S ribosomes are responsible for protein synthesis c) Its DNA is coiled around histone proteins d) It have prominent rER [printed key: B]

# Item

## label
Mycoplasma membrane contains cholesterol

## id
CON-INF-7CCB09F434AF06

## canonical_key
mycoplasma.cell-membrane.contains-sterol

## definition
**Mycoplasma** differs from every other prokaryote in that its cytoplasmic membrane __contains cholesterol__, a sterol scavenged from the host cell.

This sterol stabilises the membrane in the **permanent absence of a peptidoglycan cell wall**. Mycoplasma remains a prokaryote in every other respect: no mitochondria, and still **70S ribosomes**.

## explicit_objective
Identify cholesterol in the cell membrane as the feature that sets Mycoplasma apart from the rest of the prokaryotic cells.

## concept_type
classification

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids

1

## exam_weight_by_year
ASU_Y1=0.25

## clinical_relevance
0.4

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
General bacteriology

## subtopic
Cell-wall-deficient bacteria

## microtopic
Mycoplasma and L-forms

## nanotopic

## aliases
Mycoplasma membrane cholesterol
Sterol-containing bacterial membrane

## pitfalls
Believing Mycoplasma uses mitochondria for respiration or 80S ribosomes for protein synthesis because it "behaves" like a more complex organism. Mycoplasma is still a prokaryote — no mitochondria, still 70S ribosomes — and the only genuine difference tested here is the sterol-containing membrane.

ART-INF-MYCOPLASMA-L-FORMS

# Item

## label
Gram-negative cell wall composition

## id
CON-INF-0DD46C0FD80938

## canonical_key
bacteria.gram-negative-cell-wall.composition

## definition
The **Gram-negative cell wall** is a __thin peptidoglycan layer__ sandwiched between the cytoplasmic membrane and an **outer membrane** that carries **lipopolysaccharide (LPS)**.

The **periplasmic space** between the two membranes holds transport and hydrolytic proteins.

**Teichoic acid** is not a Gram-negative wall component — it belongs to the Gram-positive wall.

## explicit_objective
List the genuine components of the Gram-negative cell wall (thin peptidoglycan, outer LPS membrane, periplasmic proteins) and exclude teichoic acid as the false statement among them.

## concept_type
classification

## status
under review

General bacteriology

## subtopic
Bacterial cell envelope

## microtopic
Cell wall and outer membrane

## nanotopic

## aliases
Gram-negative cell wall composition
Gram-negative bacterial envelope

## pitfalls
Assuming teichoic acid is a universal cell-wall component because it sounds like a generic structural molecule. It is restricted to the Gram-positive wall, which has no outer membrane to test the same idea against.

ART-INF-CELL-WALL-OUTER-MEMBRANE

# Item

## label
Mycoplasma vs L-forms: permanent vs induced wall loss

## id
CON-INF-271E9930B4B73A

## canonical_key
mycoplasma.vs-l-forms.genetic-vs-induced-wall-loss

## definition
**Mycoplasma** is genetically incapable of synthesising peptidoglycan and so __permanently lacks a cell wall__.

**L-forms** are cell-wall-deficient variants of otherwise wall-forming bacteria, produced when a chemical reagent, antibiotic (for example a beta-lactam) or **lysozyme** removes the wall. Unlike Mycoplasma's permanent state, __an L-form's wall loss is induced__ and the underlying species retains the genes to make one.

## explicit_objective
Distinguish Mycoplasma's permanent, genetically determined absence of a cell wall from an L-form's reagent- or enzyme-induced wall loss.

## concept_type
classification

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

0.82

## topic
General bacteriology

## subtopic
Cell-wall-deficient bacteria

## microtopic
Mycoplasma and L-forms

## nanotopic

## aliases
Mycoplasma vs L-forms
Genetic vs induced cell-wall loss

## pitfalls
Treating Mycoplasma and L-forms as interchangeable because both currently lack a cell wall. The mechanism differs: Mycoplasma never had the genes to make one, while an L-form is an induced, potentially reversible state of an otherwise wall-competent species.

## article_ids
ART-INF-MYCOPLASMA-L-FORMS

# Item

## label
Mesosomes: membrane infoldings for respiratory enzymes

## id
CON-INF-E4012E20B5A13D

## canonical_key
bacteria.mesosome.structure-and-role

## definition
**Mesosomes** are invaginations of the bacterial cytoplasmic membrane, classically described as housing the **cytochrome (respiratory chain) enzymes**. Being membrane-derived, they contain __no sterol__.

Mesosomes are a **bacterial (prokaryotic) feature** and are not present in eukaryotic cells such as fungi.

## explicit_objective
Identify mesosomes as cytoplasmic-membrane invaginations that carry respiratory enzymes, state that they differentiate prokaryotic from eukaryotic cells, and exclude both sterol content and any presence in fungi.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

## modules
ASU-INF

## module_subject
ASU-INF > Microbiology > Lectures > General bacteriology: cell structure (Chapters 1-3)

## universities
asu

## learner_years
1

## blueprint_weight
0.45

## exam_weight_by_year
ASU_Y1=0.45

## clinical_relevance
0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
General bacteriology

## subtopic
Bacterial surface and internal structures

## microtopic
Pili, flagella, capsule and mesosomes

## nanotopic

## aliases
Bacterial mesosome
Cytoplasmic membrane invagination

## pitfalls
Placing mesosomes in fungi because both are studied in the same microbiology course. Mesosomes are a bacterial (prokaryotic) structure only; a eukaryotic fungal cell has mitochondria for the respiratory role instead. A student who reads a horizontal-sounding distractor like "present in fungi" as plausible has confused the two kingdoms.

## article_ids
ART-INF-SURFACE-APPENDAGES-MESOSOMES

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording
6- Which of the following structures is considered essential to the bacteria: a) ordinary pili b) Sex pili c) Mesosomes d) Slime layer [printed key: C]. 14- Which of the followings isn't true about mesosomes?: a. Invaginations from CM b. Doesn't contain sterol c. Are the main site of cytochrome enzymes & respiration D. Present in fungi [printed key: D]. 30-Which of the following components characterizes the prokaryotic cell and differentiates it from the eukaryotic cell? a. 80 S ribosomes b. Nuclear membrane c. Mesosomes d. Histones [printed key: C]

## conflicts
[clear]

## uncertainty
Mesosomes are widely taught at this level as a genuine organelle-like structure; some modern microbiology sources treat classically described mesosomes as artefacts of chemical fixation rather than a structure present in living cells. This batch follows the local exam's own teaching, as instructed, without asserting which view is correct.

# Item

## label
Flagella: motility organelles made of flagellin

## id
CON-INF-83707B09F53803

## canonical_key
bacteria.flagellum.flagellin-motility

## definition
The bacterial **flagellum** is the structure responsible for __motility__.

It is a helical filament built from repeating subunits of the protein **flagellin**, rotated by a **basal-body motor** to drive the cell through liquid.

## explicit_objective
Name flagellin as the protein subunit of the motility-conferring bacterial flagellum.

# Item

## label
The capsule: virulence, identification and vaccines

## id
CON-INF-25846A77987558

## canonical_key
bacteria.capsule.function-virulence-identification-vaccine

## definition
The polysaccharide (or, in **Bacillus anthracis**, polypeptide) **capsule** surrounding many bacteria is a __major virulence factor because it resists phagocytosis__.

Its antigenic specificity is also used to **identify and serotype** organisms and to prepare **capsular-polysaccharide vaccines** — such as the pneumococcal, meningococcal and Haemophilus influenzae type b vaccines.

## explicit_objective
State that the capsule functions simultaneously as a virulence factor, an organism-identification tool and a vaccine antigen.

## concept_type
mechanism

under review

## pitfalls
Picking only one of the capsule's roles (for example "just virulence") when a question asks what it is for. The capsule genuinely serves all three purposes — virulence, identification and vaccine antigen — at once, which is exactly what an "all of the above" option is testing.

# Item

## label
Peptidoglycan gives the cell wall its rigidity

## id
CON-INF-3FBC905C4F778F

## canonical_key
bacteria.cell-wall.peptidoglycan-rigidity

## definition
**Peptidoglycan (murein)** — alternating N-acetylglucosamine and N-acetylmuramic acid chains cross-linked by short peptides — forms a mesh-like **sacculus**.

This mesh __gives the bacterial cell wall its rigidity and shape__ and lets it resist osmotic lysis.

## explicit_objective
Identify peptidoglycan as the component responsible for the rigidity of the bacterial cell wall.

## concept_type
mechanism

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids

## academic_relevance

0.82

## topic
General bacteriology

## subtopic
Bacterial cell envelope

## microtopic
Cell wall and outer membrane

## nanotopic

## aliases
Peptidoglycan
Murein
Bacterial cell wall rigidity

## pitfalls
Attributing wall rigidity to the polysaccharide capsule or to lipoprotein. The capsule is a separate, often non-rigid outer layer, and lipoprotein (such as Braun's lipoprotein) anchors the outer membrane to peptidoglycan rather than providing rigidity itself.

ART-INF-CELL-WALL-OUTER-MEMBRANE

# Item

## label
Teichoic acid: a Gram-positive wall component

## id
CON-INF-7E3B831D71A008

## canonical_key
bacteria.cell-wall.gram-positive-vs-negative-components

## definition
The thick peptidoglycan wall of **Gram-positive** bacteria is threaded with wall-bound **teichoic acid** and membrane-anchored **lipoteichoic acid**, which contribute to wall charge, adhesion and immune activation.

__Gram-negative bacteria lack both.__ They instead have an outer membrane, a periplasmic space and lipopolysaccharide — none of which Gram-positive bacteria possess.

## explicit_objective
Name teichoic acid and lipoteichoic acid as components present in Gram-positive but not Gram-negative bacteria, and state the reciprocal outer-membrane/periplasmic-space/LPS components unique to Gram-negative organisms.

## concept_type
classification

## status
under review

## nanotopic

Teichoic acid vs Gram-negative envelope

## pitfalls
Reversing the comparison and placing an outer membrane, LPS or a periplasmic space on the Gram-positive side. Those three are Gram-negative-specific; the Gram-positive wall has no outer membrane at all.

# Item

## label
Gram-negative outer membrane: lipid A and porins

## id
CON-INF-BF26D7E563FB78

## canonical_key
bacteria.outer-membrane.lps-porins-function

## definition
The **outer membrane** of Gram-negative bacteria contains **lipopolysaccharide**, whose **lipid A** component is the __toxic moiety (endotoxin)__.

**Porin** proteins span the outer membrane and admit small hydrophilic solutes, but __not large molecules such as amino acids or proteins__. The outer membrane occurs only in Gram-negative bacteria, not in Gram-positive ones.

## explicit_objective
State that lipid A is toxic, that porins restrict passage to small solutes (excluding amino acids), and that the outer membrane is present only in Gram-negative bacteria.

## concept_type
mechanism

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

0.82

General bacteriology

## subtopic
Bacterial cell envelope

## microtopic
Cell wall and outer membrane

## nanotopic

## aliases
Outer membrane porins
Lipid A endotoxin

## pitfalls
Believing porins are non-selective channels that admit anything, including amino acids. Porins restrict passage by size and charge; larger or more complex solutes need dedicated transport systems, not a simple porin channel.

ART-INF-CELL-WALL-OUTER-MEMBRANE

# Item

## label
L-forms and Mycoplasma replicate without a cell wall

## id
CON-INF-D2C104EA01CE30

## canonical_key
bacteria.cell-wall-deficient-forms.replication-capable

## definition
Both **L-forms** (induced cell-wall-deficient variants of normally wall-forming bacteria) and **Mycoplasma** (a genus that never forms a cell wall) __can still replicate despite the absence of peptidoglycan__.

A true **protoplast**, by contrast, is an artificially wall-stripped cell that is typically osmotically fragile — not a stable, self-replicating clinical form.

## explicit_objective
Identify L-forms and Mycoplasma, not protoplasts, as the cell-wall-deficient bacterial forms able to replicate.

## concept_type
classification

under review

inf

## microtopic

## nanotopic

L-forms and Mycoplasma replication

## pitfalls
Including protoplasts alongside L-forms and Mycoplasma as replicating wall-deficient forms. A protoplast is an experimentally stripped cell, distinct from the naturally occurring, replication-competent L-form and Mycoplasma.

# Item

## label
Cytoplasmic membrane: location and toxin excretion

## id
CON-INF-16D6694E224671

## canonical_key
bacteria.cytoplasmic-membrane.functions

## definition
The bacterial cytoplasmic membrane sits __inside, not outside, the cell wall__. Besides transport and energy generation, it plays a role in **toxin excretion** — many exotoxins are exported across it.

It lacks sterol in every bacterial genus **except Mycoplasma**, and it is not, on its own, generally described as osmotically resistant.

## explicit_objective
State that the cytoplasmic membrane is internal to the cell wall, lacks sterol except in Mycoplasma, and participates in toxin excretion.

## concept_type
mechanism

## status
under review

inf

## academic_relevance

0.82

## topic
General bacteriology

## subtopic
Bacterial cell envelope

## microtopic
Cytoplasmic membrane and ribosomes

## nanotopic

## aliases
Cytoplasmic membrane function
Bacterial membrane toxin excretion

## pitfalls
Placing the cytoplasmic membrane outside the cell wall. It is the innermost boundary; the wall, and in Gram-negative bacteria the outer membrane too, lie external to it.

ART-INF-BACTERIAL-MEMBRANE-RIBOSOME

# Item

## label
Ordinary pili adhere; sex pili conjugate

## id
CON-INF-BC446C9816D9CE

## canonical_key
bacteria.pili.ordinary-vs-sex-pili-function

## definition
**Ordinary (common) pili** are short, hair-like appendages whose main function is __adhesion__ of the bacterium to host cells or surfaces.

**Sex pili** are a distinct, longer type used specifically for **conjugation** — the transfer of DNA between bacteria — not for motility or protection from phagocytosis.

## explicit_objective
State that the main function of ordinary pili is adhesion, and distinguish this from the gene-transfer role of sex pili.

## concept_type
classification

## status
under review

## subject
inf

General bacteriology

## subtopic
Bacterial surface and internal structures

## microtopic
Pili, flagella, capsule and mesosomes

## nanotopic

## aliases
Ordinary pili function
Sex pili conjugation

## pitfalls
Assigning motility to pili. That is the flagellum's job — a pilus is rigid and non-rotating; it adheres (ordinary pili) or transfers DNA (sex pili), it does not propel the cell.

# Item

## label
Growth profile of pathogenic bacteria

## id
CON-INF-3528D1EAF3F9B4

## canonical_key
bacteria.growth-requirements.pathogen-profile

## definition
Most pathogenic bacteria are **heterotrophic** (using organic carbon sources), **facultatively anaerobic** (growing with or without oxygen) and **mesophilic** (optimal growth around body temperature).

They are __undemanding for CO2__, needing only the ~**0.03%** present in ordinary air rather than an enriched atmosphere.

## explicit_objective
State the combined heterotrophic / facultative anaerobic / mesophilic / ~0.03% CO2 profile that describes most pathogenic bacteria.

## concept_type
classification

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids

## universities

## exam_weight_by_year

## clinical_relevance

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
General bacteriology

## subtopic
Bacterial growth and nutrition

## microtopic
Nutritional and environmental requirements

## nanotopic

## aliases
Typical pathogen growth profile
Pathogenic bacteria growth requirements

## pitfalls
Assuming pathogens are typically obligate aerobes or true capnophiles requiring an enriched CO2 atmosphere. Most pathogens are metabolically flexible facultative anaerobes that grow adequately at ordinary atmospheric CO2, not the elevated CO2 that genuine capnophiles such as Neisseria or Brucella need.

# Item

## label
Exacting (fastidious) bacteria need organic C and N

## id
CON-INF-9386E80307EC94

## canonical_key
bacteria.nutrition.exacting-fastidious-requirements

## definition
**Exacting (fastidious)** bacteria are heterotrophs with more demanding nutritional needs: they require an __organic source for both carbon and nitrogen__.

Because they cannot fix carbon from CO2 or use an inorganic nitrogen source, they typically need **enriched media** to grow.

## explicit_objective
State that exacting/fastidious bacteria are heterotrophs requiring an organic source for both carbon and nitrogen.

## concept_type
classification

Mixing up which element needs to be organic. A fastidious organism needs BOTH carbon and nitrogen from organic sources, not just one — picking "organic C, inorganic N" or "inorganic C, organic N" is only half right.

# Item

## label
Environmental classes: halophilic, capnophilic, thermophilic, microaerophilic

## id
CON-INF-3D845DC6B07880

## canonical_key
bacteria.environmental-classification.halophilic-capnophilic-thermophilic-microaerophilic

## definition
Bacteria are grouped by environmental tolerance: **halophilic** tolerate high NaCl; **capnophilic** require increased CO2; **thermophilic** prefer high growth temperatures; and **microaerophilic** prefer only a small amount of oxygen.

__Each term names a distinct tolerance__, not an interchangeable synonym for the others.

## explicit_objective
Match each environmental-tolerance term (halophilic, capnophilic, thermophilic, microaerophilic) to the condition it names, starting with high-NaCl tolerance as halophilic.

classification

## pitfalls
Confusing halophilic (salt-tolerant) with capnophilic (CO2-requiring) because both are less-familiar terms learned in the same list. They describe entirely different environmental axes — salinity versus gas composition.

# Item

## label
Catalase and SOD detoxify oxygen; obligate anaerobes lack them

## id
CON-INF-2E9D6F833A7D5E

## canonical_key
bacteria.oxygen-detox-enzymes.catalase-sod

## definition
Bacteria using **aerobic respiration** generate toxic **reactive oxygen species** — superoxide and hydrogen peroxide — and need **catalase** and/or **superoxide dismutase (SOD)** to detoxify them.

__Obligate anaerobes are the one group that lacks these enzymes__, which is why oxygen is toxic to them. Aerobes, facultative anaerobes and microaerophiles all retain at least one of catalase or SOD.

## explicit_objective
State that catalase and/or superoxide dismutase are required for aerobic respiration, and identify obligate anaerobes as the group that lacks ROS-degrading enzymes.

## concept_type
mechanism

## status
under review

## pitfalls
Naming alkaline phosphatase as the oxygen-detoxifying enzyme. It has no role in reactive-oxygen-species defence; that role belongs specifically to catalase and superoxide dismutase.

# Item

## label
Treponema pallidum cannot be grown on artificial media

## id
CON-INF-F73611037EBC24

## canonical_key
bacteria.non-cultivable-species.treponema-pallidum

## definition
**Treponema pallidum**, the spirochaete that causes **syphilis**, __cannot be isolated or grown on artificial culture media__ in the clinical laboratory.

This sets it apart from Mycobacterium tuberculosis, Bacillus anthracis and Corynebacterium diphtheriae, which are all culturable. Diagnosis instead relies on **dark-field microscopy and serology**.

## explicit_objective
Identify Treponema pallidum as an organism that cannot be isolated on culture media, in contrast to M. tuberculosis, B. anthracis and C. diphtheriae, which can.

## pitfalls

# Item

## label
Bacterial growth curve: the four phases

## id
CON-INF-6D56F46B5F1EF4

## canonical_key
bacteria.growth-curve.phase-definitions

## definition
The bacterial growth curve has four phases: **lag** (adaptation, no net cell division), **exponential/log** (maximal division rate), **stationary** (new cells equal dying cells, so the population plateaus) and **decline/death** (dying cells exceed those formed).

Both lag and stationary appear as horizontal lines, but they differ: __in lag no cell division occurs at all__, whereas the stationary phase reflects dividing and dying cells in balance.

## explicit_objective
Explain why the lag phase differs from the stationary phase despite both appearing as plateaus on the growth curve: no division at all, versus balanced division and death.

## concept_type
mechanism

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

## modules
ASU-INF

## module_subject
ASU-INF > Microbiology > Lectures > General bacteriology: growth, nutrition and oxygen requirements (Chapters 1-3)

## universities
asu

## learner_years
1

## blueprint_weight
0.35

## exam_weight_by_year
ASU_Y1=0.35

## clinical_relevance
0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
General bacteriology

## subtopic
Bacterial growth and nutrition

## microtopic
The bacterial growth curve

## nanotopic

## aliases
Bacterial growth curve phases
Lag phase vs stationary phase

## pitfalls
Reading a horizontal line on the growth curve as always meaning "no activity". During the stationary phase cells are still dividing, just as fast as others are dying; only the lag phase is a true state of no division.

## article_ids
ART-INF-GROWTH-CURVE

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording
22- both lag and stationary phases are represented by horizontal lines in growth curve, however lag phase is different in: a) no cell division occur b) The number of dying cells is equal to the number of generated cells c) the number of dying cells exceeds the number of formed cells d) the rate of bacterial division is higher [printed key: A]. 29- What is the main event characterizing the phase (A) in the corresponding figure of bacterial growth curve?: a. Adaptation of the bacteria to the new environment b. Germination of spore forming bacteria c. Increased susceptibility to antibiotics d. Depletion of nutrients [printed key: A]

# Item

## label
Autotrophic vs heterotrophic bacteria

## id
CON-INF-841FAB7F11BA18

## canonical_key
bacteria.nutrition.autotrophic-vs-heterotrophic

## definition
**Autotrophic** bacteria synthesise organic molecules using **CO2** as their carbon source and __do not cause human disease__.

**Heterotrophic** bacteria require preformed organic carbon and include the parasitic, pathogenic and normal-flora (commensal) organisms that matter clinically — __every medically relevant bacterium is heterotrophic__.

## explicit_objective
State that autotrophs use CO2 as a carbon source and are not pathogens, while heterotrophic bacteria (parasitic, pathogenic and flora) are the clinically relevant group.

## concept_type
classification

under review

## pitfalls

# Item

## label
Bacteria are most antibiotic-sensitive in log phase

## id
CON-INF-2E629B81136A05

## canonical_key
bacteria.growth-curve.phase-antibiotic-sensitivity

## definition
Antibiotics that target actively growing or dividing cells — such as **beta-lactams** acting on cell-wall synthesis — are __most effective during the exponential (log) phase__, when division and metabolic activity peak.

Bacteria in the lag, stationary or decline phase are relatively less susceptible.

## explicit_objective
State that maximal antibiotic sensitivity occurs in the exponential growth phase.

## concept_type
clinical_feature

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

## academic_relevance

0.82

## topic
General bacteriology

## subtopic
Bacterial growth and nutrition

## microtopic
The bacterial growth curve

## nanotopic

## aliases
Antibiotic sensitivity by growth phase
Exponential phase antibiotic susceptibility

## pitfalls
Assuming the stationary phase, where the population is largest, is also when antibiotics work best. Susceptibility tracks active growth and division, which peaks in the exponential phase, not simply the size of the population.

ART-INF-GROWTH-CURVE

direct_statement

# Item

## label
Oxygen requirement classes of bacteria

## id
CON-INF-D31966C6CEF85C

## canonical_key
bacteria.oxygen-requirement-classes

## definition
**Facultative anaerobes** grow with or without oxygen, switching their metabolism accordingly.

**Obligate aerobes** require oxygen, **obligate anaerobes** are killed or fail to grow in its presence, and **microaerophiles** grow best with only __trace amounts of oxygen__ rather than the full atmospheric concentration.

## explicit_objective
Distinguish facultative anaerobes (grow with or without O2) from microaerophiles (need only traces of O2), obligate aerobes and obligate anaerobes.

## concept_type
classification

## status

Oxygen requirement classes of bacteria
Facultative anaerobe vs microaerophile

## pitfalls
Confusing microaerophilic with facultative anaerobic. A facultative anaerobe tolerates a full range from no oxygen to normal atmospheric oxygen, whereas a microaerophile specifically needs a small amount and does not thrive at full atmospheric concentration.

Facultative anaerobes [printed key: C]

# Item

## label
What determines the length of the lag phase

## id
CON-INF-73C7354E3479CD

## canonical_key
bacteria.growth-curve.lag-period-determinants

## definition
The length of the **lag phase** depends on the __type of organism__ and the __type of media__ (and prior conditions) it is inoculated into — for example, an organism moved from a rich medium to a poor one, or transferred from a different growth phase, shows a longer lag.

**Incubation temperature** affects growth rate throughout the curve rather than being the specific determinant of lag duration here.

## explicit_objective
State that lag-period length is determined by organism type and media type together.

## concept_type
mechanism

## status
under review

inf

0.82

## topic
General bacteriology

## subtopic
Bacterial growth and nutrition

## microtopic
The bacterial growth curve

## nanotopic

## aliases
Lag period determinants
Factors determining lag phase length

## pitfalls
Picking temperature of incubation alone as the determinant of lag length. Temperature affects growth rate throughout the curve, but the specific factors named for lag duration are the organism's own physiology and the medium it is inoculated into.

Confusing invasins, which trigger receptor-driven uptake into host cells, with enzymes such as hyaluronidase that spread bacteria between tissues, or with capsule/protein A, which block phagocytosis rather than promote cell entry.

## article_ids
ART-INF-BACTERIAL-INVASION-PATHOGENICITY

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording
2-invasins are proteins that help in bacterial invasion by: a) inducing apoptosis of epithelial barrier cells b) breaking down extracellular matrix of connective tissue c) preventing phagocytosis by immunity d) interacting with specific receptors on the host cells, causing intracellular uptake [printed key: D]

# Item
## label
Disease depends on both virulence and host immunity

## id
CON-INF-A6DFAC5D14B3D4

## canonical_key
bacteria.pathogenicity.determinants

## definition
Whether an organism actually causes disease depends __jointly on the pathogen's virulence__ (its capacity to invade and damage tissue) __and the state of the host's immune defenses__.

Neither factor acts in isolation.

## explicit_objective
State that disease-causing ability (pathogenicity) reflects the balance between organism virulence and host immunity.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

Bacterial virulence and genetics

## subtopic
Bacterial invasion and pathogenicity

## microtopic
Pathogenicity determinants

## nanotopic

## aliases
Pathogenicity determinants
Host-pathogen balance

## pitfalls
Treating pathogenicity as a fixed property of the organism alone; an immunocompromised host can develop disease from a low-virulence organism, and a robust host can resist a highly virulent one.

ART-INF-BACTERIAL-INVASION-PATHOGENICITY

# Item
## label
Parasitism, commensalism and mutualism

## id
CON-INF-0B4BAFFD525FDF

## canonical_key
bacteria.host-relationships.parasitism-commensalism-mutualism

## definition
In **parasitism** the microbe benefits at the expense of the host (true of pathogenic bacteria).

In **commensalism** the microbe benefits while the host is neither helped nor harmed.

In **mutualism** __both partners benefit__.

## explicit_objective
Differentiate parasitism, commensalism and mutualism by whether the host is harmed, unaffected, or benefited by the relationship.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

## subtopic
Host-microbe relationships and normal flora

## microtopic
Symbiotic relationship types

## nanotopic

## aliases
Symbiotic relationship types
Parasitism vs commensalism vs mutualism

## pitfalls
Confusing commensalism (host unaffected, microbe benefits) with mutualism (both partners benefit) -- pathogenic bacteria are classed as parasites, not as any form of symbiont that benefits the host.

## article_ids
ART-INF-HOST-RELATIONSHIPS-NORMAL-FLORA

direct_statement

Also: 7- in commensalism relationship: a) host gets benefit, while the microbe doesn't b) both host and microbe get benefit c) microbe gets benefit while the host isn't harmed d) microbe gets benefit while the host is harmed [printed key: C]. Also: 22- What is the type of symbiotic relation that benefits both microbe & host? a) Mutual relationship b) Parasitic relationship c) Commensal relationship d) Autotrophic [printed key: A]

# Item
## label
Exotoxin vs endotoxin

## id
CON-INF-C87DF729E2ADDF

## canonical_key
bacteria.toxins.exotoxin-vs-endotoxin

## definition
An **exotoxin** is a polypeptide or protein __actively secreted by living bacteria__ (Gram-positive or Gram-negative).

This distinguishes it from **endotoxin** — the lipopolysaccharide/lipid A structural component of the Gram-negative outer membrane, __released mainly on bacterial lysis__ rather than secreted.

## explicit_objective
State that an exotoxin is a secreted polypeptide/protein toxin, as opposed to the lipid-based structural nature of endotoxin.

## concept_type
definition

## nanotopic

## aliases

Protein toxin

## pitfalls

# Item
## label
Formalin turns exotoxin into a toxoid vaccine

## id
CON-INF-F6DC0E99178186

## canonical_key
bacteria.toxoid.formalin-inactivation

## definition
Treating an AB-subunit exotoxin such as **diphtheria toxin** with **formalin** __destroys its toxicity while preserving its antigenicity__, producing a **toxoid** used as a vaccine.

Structural components such as lipid A or teichoic acid are not toxoided this way.

## explicit_objective
State that formalin treatment of an AB exotoxin (e.g. diphtheria toxin) produces an immunogenic, non-toxic toxoid.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## pitfalls
Assuming toxoid formation works on any bacterial toxic component; the classic examinable example is an AB-subunit exotoxin (diphtheria), not endotoxin (lipid A) or teichoic acid, neither of which is toxoided this way.

# Item
## label
Septic shock is caused by lipid A of LPS

## id
CON-INF-D60EAAF763C476

## canonical_key
bacteria.septic-shock.lps-mediated-pathogenesis

## definition
Septic shock following Gram-negative infection is caused by **lipopolysaccharide**, specifically its **lipid A** component, which triggers a __massive host cytokine response__ — fever, hypotension and multi-organ dysfunction.

It is not caused by an AB exotoxin, a superantigen, or a type III hypersensitivity reaction.

## explicit_objective
State that lipid A of LPS is the cause of septic shock in Gram-negative sepsis, and recognize the clinical picture of fever and hypotension after Gram-negative infection.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

Exotoxins, endotoxin and shock states

## microtopic
Septic shock pathogenesis

## nanotopic

## aliases
LPS-induced septic shock
Lipid A pathogenesis

## pitfalls
Attributing septic shock to a secreted exotoxin, a superantigen, or a hypersensitivity mechanism; in Gram-negative sepsis the trigger is the structural lipid A of LPS released from the bacterial cell wall.

Also: 20- A 27-year-old man had an operation, and the wound becomes infected by a gram-ve organism, after 4 hours, he developed headache, muscle aches. His body temperature was 40 C., and his blood pressure was 80/50 mmHg. This patient's condition is likely to be caused by which of the following? a) Protein A b) capsule c) toxin that has A and B subunits d) Lipopolysaccharide [printed key: D]

# Item
## label
Hemolysins are membrane-disturbing cytolytic toxins

## id
CON-INF-2A6CE8EF5F40F7

## canonical_key
bacteria.toxins.hemolysin-mechanism

## definition
**Hemolysins** are bacterial exotoxins that damage host-cell membranes, including red blood cells, by __directly disrupting membrane integrity__.

This makes them **membrane-disturbing cytolytic toxins** — not endotoxins, structural toxins, or AB-subunit toxins.

## explicit_objective
Classify hemolysins as membrane-disturbing cytolytic toxins rather than endotoxins or AB-subunit toxins.

# Item
## label
Teichoic acid drives Gram-positive inflammation

## id
CON-INF-22F2937E8D094E

## canonical_key
bacteria.teichoic-acid.gram-positive-pathogenesis-role

## definition
**Teichoic acid**, a cell-wall component unique to Gram-positive bacteria, contributes to pathogenesis by __inducing chemotaxis of inflammatory cells and triggering release of inflammatory mediators__.

It functions as the **Gram-positive counterpart to LPS** in provoking a host inflammatory response, rather than acting as an endotoxin or superantigen itself.

## explicit_objective
State that teichoic acid's pathogenic role is to induce chemotaxis and mediator release, distinct from endotoxin or superantigen action.

## concept_type
definition

## pitfalls
Calling teichoic acid an endotoxin or a superantigen; it is a structural Gram-positive wall component whose pathogenic role is inducing chemotaxis and mediator release, a distinct mechanism from either.

# Item
## label
TSST-1: the staphylococcal superantigen

## id
CON-INF-6F8D6DE961F269

## canonical_key
staph-aureus.tsst-1.superantigen-mechanism

## definition
**Toxic shock syndrome toxin-1 (TSST-1)**, produced by *S. aureus*, is a **superantigen** that __non-specifically cross-links MHC class II molecules and T-cell receptors__, causing massive, unrestricted T-cell activation and a cytokine storm.

It is the toxin responsible for staphylococcal toxic shock syndrome, is released during active bacterial growth rather than only on death, and does not itself disturb protein synthesis.

## explicit_objective
State that TSST-1 acts as a superantigen and is the toxin responsible for staphylococcal toxic shock syndrome.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

## subtopic

## microtopic
TSST-1 superantigen mechanism

## nanotopic

## aliases
Toxic shock syndrome toxin-1
Staphylococcal superantigen

## pitfalls
Confusing a superantigen's non-specific, unrestricted T-cell activation with a conventional antigen's specific peptide-MHC-TCR interaction, or assuming TSST-1 must disturb protein synthesis (a mechanism belonging to AB exotoxins such as diphtheria toxin, not to superantigens).

## article_ids
ART-INF-MEMBRANE-TOXINS-SUPERANTIGENS

direct_statement

# Item
## label
Toxic shock vs septic shock: shared features

## id
CON-INF-F2B1AF50F8D51C

## canonical_key
bacteria.toxic-vs-septic-shock.shared-features

## definition
**Toxic shock syndrome** (superantigen-mediated, *S. aureus*) and **septic shock** (LPS-mediated, Gram-negative sepsis) share overproduction of mediators and cytokines (**TNF-alpha, IL-1, IL-6**), fever and hypotension.

Both are mediated by bacterial toxins or wall components — __neither requires live circulating bacteria__ to be present at the time of shock.

## explicit_objective
Recognize the features shared by toxic and septic shock, and identify that being "caused by living bacteria" is not one of them since both are toxin/mediator-driven states.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## exam_weight_by_year
ASU_Y1=0.25

## clinical_relevance
0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
Bacterial virulence and genetics

## subtopic
Exotoxins, endotoxin and shock states

## microtopic
Toxic vs septic shock overlap

## nanotopic

## aliases
Shock-state overlap
Toxic vs septic shock

## pitfalls
Assuming systemic shock always implies ongoing bacteremia; both toxic and septic shock are driven by toxin/mediator effects already released, independent of a live organism burden at the time of shock.

## article_ids
ART-INF-EXOTOXIN-ENDOTOXIN-SHOCK

## support_mode
direct_statement

14- All of the following is common between toxic shock and septic shock except: a) overproduction of mediators and cytokines "TNF-alfa, IL-1, IL-6" b) fever, hypotension c) caused by by living bacteria d) mediated by bacterial toxins [printed key: C]

# Item
## label
The lower respiratory tract is normally sterile

## id
CON-INF-3E10BA25A4998E

## canonical_key

The **bronchi and lower respiratory tract** are __normally free of resident microbial flora__.

This contrasts with the tissue around the lymphatic system, the GI tract and the skin, all of which carry normal flora.

# Item
## label
Functions of normal flora

## id
CON-INF-C8EE841CE9808D

## canonical_key
microbiome.normal-flora.functions

## definition
**Normal (resident) flora** benefit the host by producing some **vitamins**, forming part of **innate immunity**, and providing a strong stimulus for the developing immune system of an infant.

__Producing powerful toxins against the host is not a function of normal flora.__

## explicit_objective
List the protective functions of normal flora (vitamin production, innate immunity, infant immune stimulation) and recognize that toxin production against the host is not one of them.

definition

Functions of normal flora

# Item
## label
Invasion factors vs pili and flagella

## id
CON-INF-1EAFF70A6FC769

## canonical_key
bacteria.virulence-factors.collagenase-hyaluronidase-invasins-vs-pili

## definition
**Spreading/invasion factors** actively promote bacterial invasion of tissue: **collagenase** and **hyaluronidase** break down connective-tissue matrix, and **invasins** trigger cellular uptake.

__Ordinary pili (adhesion) and flagella (motility) are not invasion-promoting factors__ — they serve other functions.

## explicit_objective
Rank collagenase, hyaluronidase and invasins as invasion-promoting factors, distinct from ordinary pili and flagella, whose roles are adhesion and motility.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

## modules
ASU-INF

ASU-INF > Microbiology > Lectures > Bacterial virulence, toxins and genetics (Chapters 4-5)

## universities
asu

## learner_years
1

## blueprint_weight
0.25

## exam_weight_by_year
ASU_Y1=0.25

## clinical_relevance
0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
Bacterial virulence and genetics

## subtopic
Bacterial invasion and pathogenicity

## microtopic
Spreading factors vs adhesion/motility structures

## nanotopic

## aliases
Spreading factors
Invasion-promoting enzymes

## pitfalls
Assuming every structure a bacterium uses to interact with host tissue also drives invasion; ordinary pili mediate attachment and flagella mediate motility, neither of which breaks down tissue barriers or triggers cellular uptake the way collagenase, hyaluronidase and invasins do.

## article_ids
ART-INF-BACTERIAL-INVASION-PATHOGENICITY

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording

# Item
## label
Phospholipase disrupts membranes enzymatically

## id
CON-INF-CFC7896BF71239

## canonical_key
bacteria.phospholipase-hemolysin.mechanism

## definition
**Phospholipase** (also called hemolysin in this context) is a protein that __disrupts host-cell membranes by enzymatic hydrolysis of membrane phospholipids__.

This mechanism is distinct from and unrelated to the superantigen mechanism that induces toxic shock syndrome.

## explicit_objective
Identify phospholipase as a membrane-disrupting protein enzyme, and recognize that inducing toxic shock is not one of its properties (that mechanism belongs to superantigens such as TSST-1).

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## pitfalls

# Item
## label
Injectisomes (Type III secretion) inject toxins into cells

## id
CON-INF-4CDBD486C53EB4

## canonical_key
bacteria.type-iii-secretion-system.injectisomes

## definition
The **Type III secretion system**, structurally an **injectisome**, is a needle-like apparatus found mostly in Gram-negative bacteria.

It __injects effector proteins/toxins directly from the bacterial cytoplasm into the host-cell cytoplasm__, aiding invasion and pathogenicity by this direct-delivery route rather than by extracellular release.

## explicit_objective
State that injectisomes (Type III secretion systems) deliver toxins directly into host cells, contributing to invasion and pathogenicity, rather than releasing toxins extracellularly.

definition

# Item
## label
Transposons: jumping genes

## id
CON-INF-D6A264E108B348

## canonical_key
bacteria.genetics.transposons

## definition
**Transposons** are DNA segments capable of __moving from one location to another__ — within the genome or between chromosome and plasmid — popularly termed "jumping genes."

They are distinct from conjugative plasmids, sex-pilus genes, or integrated non-lytic (lysogenic) phage.

## explicit_objective
Identify transposons as mobile genetic elements ("jumping genes") able to relocate within or between DNA molecules.

## concept_type
definition

# Item
## label
Phenotypic variation is reversible; genotypic is heritable

## id
CON-INF-3576B51A9E5B1B

## canonical_key
bacteria.genetics.phenotypic-vs-genotypic-variation

## definition
**Phenotypic variation** — for example spore formation and vegetative reversion — is a __reversible, non-heritable change__ in expressed characteristics.

**Genotypic variation** is heritable, DNA-sequence-level change (such as mutation). The two are distinguished by reversibility, not by heritability, environmental independence, or spontaneity of origin.

## explicit_objective
Differentiate reversible phenotypic variation from heritable genotypic variation, using spore formation/vegetation as the phenotypic example and mutation as the genotypic one.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## subtopic

## microtopic
Phenotypic vs genotypic variation

Reversible bacterial variation

## pitfalls
Treating any observable change in a bacterium as evidence of a genetic mutation; a reversible, environmentally triggered change such as sporulation and its reversal (vegetation) is phenotypic, not genotypic.

# Item
## label
Generalized vs specialized transduction

## id
CON-INF-E9C14F5981ACE5

## canonical_key
bacteria.genetics.transduction-generalized-vs-specialized

## definition
**Transduction** is bacteriophage-mediated gene transfer, occurring in both Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacteria.

In **generalized transduction** (a virulent/lytic phage) __random host DNA is accidentally packaged and transferred__ — including an otherwise non-self-transmissible plasmid, such as a Gram-positive beta-lactamase plasmid.

In **specialized transduction** (a lysogenic/temperate phage) only DNA adjacent to the prophage's integration site is transferred, including genes coded by a resident plasmid near that site.

## explicit_objective
Distinguish generalized transduction (virulent-phage-mediated, can move an otherwise non-transmissible plasmid, not restricted to Gram-negative bacteria) from specialized transduction (lysogenic-phage-mediated, transfers DNA adjacent to the prophage site).

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

0.82

## topic
Bacterial virulence and genetics

## subtopic
Bacterial genetics I -- variation and gene transfer

## microtopic
Generalized vs specialized transduction

## nanotopic

## aliases
Generalized vs specialized transduction
Phage-mediated gene transfer

## pitfalls
Assuming transduction is restricted to Gram-negative bacteria, or reversing which phage type mediates which transduction category -- generalized transduction uses a virulent (lytic) phage, specialized transduction uses a lysogenic (temperate) phage.

## article_ids
ART-INF-BACTERIAL-GENETICS-VARIATION-TRANSFER

## support_mode
direct_statement

26- Concerning Transduction, Which of the following is correct: a) A method of gene transfer between gram-ve bacteria only b) In generalized transduction, non transmissible plasmids of Gram+ve bacteria can be transferred c) specialized transduction is mediated by virulent phage d) lysogenic phage can transfer genes coded by plasmid [printed key: B]. Also: 29- B lactamase plasmid of staphylococci can be transferred by which of the following? a. Conjugation b. Specialized Transduction c. Transformation d. Generalized transduction [printed key: C&D]. Also: 35- in Gram +ve bacteria, plasmids coding for B lactamase can be transferred by a. Conjugation & generalized transduction b. Conjugation & specialized transduction c. Conjugation & transformation d. Transformation & generalized transduction e. Transformation & specialized transduction [printed key: D]

# Item
## label
Endotoxin is chromosomally encoded, not plasmid-coded

## id
CON-INF-EB5758CBC71F57

## canonical_key
bacteria.genetics.plasmid-vs-chromosome-encoded-traits

## definition
Sex-pili production, exotoxin production and ordinary-pili production can all be **plasmid-mediated**.

**Endotoxin (lipopolysaccharide)**, by contrast, is an intrinsic structural component of the Gram-negative cell wall __encoded by chromosomal genes, not carried on a plasmid__.

## explicit_objective
State that LPS/endotoxin production is a chromosomally encoded structural trait, distinguishing it from plasmid-mediated traits such as sex pili and exotoxin production.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

Bacterial virulence and genetics

## subtopic
Bacterial genetics I -- variation and gene transfer

## microtopic
Plasmid- vs chromosome-encoded traits

## nanotopic

## aliases
Plasmid-encoded vs chromosome-encoded traits
Endotoxin genetics

## pitfalls
Assuming every major bacterial virulence trait can be plasmid-mediated; endotoxin is a fixed structural feature of the Gram-negative outer membrane, encoded chromosomally, unlike many resistance and exotoxin genes.

# Item
## label
Conjugation: sex pili, F plasmid, live donor

## id
CON-INF-B7F47EC7A8F7FA

## canonical_key
bacteria.genetics.conjugation-mechanism

## definition
**Conjugation** transfers DNA (typically an **F plasmid**) from a live donor to a recipient through __direct cell contact mediated by sex pili__, with **helicase** unwinding the DNA for transfer.

The donor bacterium remains alive throughout, and the process __does not require the recipient to be "competent"__ — competence is specific to transformation.

## explicit_objective
State that conjugation requires sex pili, an F plasmid and helicase, keeps the donor alive throughout, and does not require recipient competence.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## subtopic
Bacterial genetics II -- conjugation, transformation and lysogeny

Conjugation mechanism

## nanotopic

Bacterial conjugation
Sex pilus-mediated gene transfer

## pitfalls
Confusing conjugation's requirements (sex pili, F plasmid, helicase, a living donor) with transformation's requirement (recipient competence) -- competence belongs to transformation, not conjugation.

## article_ids
ART-INF-BACTERIAL-GENETICS-CONJUGATION-LYSOGENY

# Item
## label
Transformation: competence and DNA homology

## id
CON-INF-42D77BF4AB3ADD

## canonical_key
bacteria.genetics.transformation-mechanism

## definition
**Transformation** is the uptake by a recipient bacterium of __free (soluble) DNA__ released from a donor cell.

It requires the recipient to be in a physiologically **"competent"** state and requires **sequence homology** between the incoming DNA and the recipient's genome for stable incorporation. It does not involve a phage, sex pilus or restriction endonuclease as the uptake mechanism.

## explicit_objective
State that transformation requires both recipient competence and DNA homology for uptake and incorporation of soluble extracellular DNA.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

## universities

1

## exam_weight_by_year
ASU_Y1=0.25

## clinical_relevance
0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
Bacterial virulence and genetics

## subtopic
Bacterial genetics II -- conjugation, transformation and lysogeny

## microtopic
Transformation mechanism

## nanotopic

## aliases
Bacterial transformation
Competence and DNA uptake

## pitfalls
Assuming any bacterium can take up environmental DNA at any time, or that a phage or sex pilus is required for transformation; only a competent recipient can take up soluble DNA, and stable incorporation needs homology with the recipient genome.

## article_ids
ART-INF-BACTERIAL-GENETICS-CONJUGATION-LYSOGENY

direct_statement

# Item
## label
Lysogenic conversion: new phenotype from prophage genes

## id
CON-INF-BFFA74CFBB0B25

## canonical_key
bacteria.genetics.lysogenic-conversion

## definition
**Lysogenic conversion** occurs when a temperate bacteriophage integrates its genome as a **prophage** into the bacterial chromosome.

__Genes carried by the prophage alter the host bacterium's phenotype.__ It is a change in bacterial characters due to the phage's own genes plus incorporation of a prophage — not the uptake of free environmental DNA.

## explicit_objective
State that lysogenic conversion is a phenotype change driven by phage-gene expression following prophage incorporation into the host chromosome.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

Prophage-mediated phenotype change

## pitfalls
Confusing lysogenic conversion (a phage integrating and altering phenotype via its own genes) with transformation (uptake of free environmental DNA) or with simple conversion of a virulent phage into a lysogenic one.

## article_ids
ART-INF-BACTERIAL-GENETICS-CONJUGATION-LYSOGENY

direct_statement

# Item
## label
S. saprophyticus: novobiocin resistance

## id
CON-INF-C0123F8DDE0DAA

## canonical_key

## definition
**Staphylococcus saprophyticus** is distinguished from other coagulase-negative staphylococci, such as *S. epidermidis*, by its __resistance to novobiocin__.

The novobiocin test is used specifically for species-level identification within the coagulase-negative staphylococci.

## explicit_objective

# Item
## label
M. tuberculosis: mycolic acid and acid-fastness

## id
CON-INF-AF38B9D7F0419D

## canonical_key
mycobacterium-tuberculosis.cell-wall.mycolic-acid-acid-fastness

## definition
**Mycobacterium tuberculosis** has an unusually thick cell wall with a high **mycolic acid** (long-chain fatty acid) content.

This confers __acid-fastness__ — resistance to decolorization by acid-alcohol after carbol-fuchsin staining — rather than a standard Gram-stain reaction.

## explicit_objective
State that M. tuberculosis's thick, mycolic-acid-rich cell wall is responsible for its acid-fast staining property, distinct from Gram staining.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids

Staining limitations and acid-fast/spore-forming organisms

## microtopic
Mycobacterial cell wall and acid-fastness

## nanotopic

## aliases
Acid-fast bacilli
Mycolic acid cell wall

## pitfalls
Describing M. tuberculosis by a Gram-stain color (violet or red) or claiming it resists decolorization by alcohol alone; acid-fastness specifically means resistance to decolorization by acid-alcohol after Ziehl-Neelsen staining, not a Gram-stain result.

ART-INF-STAINING-LIMITATIONS-SPORE-FORMERS

direct_statement

# Item
## label
Helicobacter pylori is not an Enterobacteriaceae

## id
CON-INF-1A6688E780A1B1

## canonical_key
enterobacteriaceae.family-membership

## definition
**Klebsiella**, **Proteus** and **Salmonella typhi** are members of the family **Enterobacteriaceae** — Gram-negative, facultatively anaerobic rods of the GI tract.

**Helicobacter pylori** is a separate genus of curved/spiral Gram-negative organism found in the gastric mucosa and __is not a member of this family__.

## explicit_objective
Identify Helicobacter pylori as not belonging to the family Enterobacteriaceae, distinguishing it from Klebsiella, Proteus and Salmonella.

## concept_type
definition

under review

## pitfalls
Assuming every clinically important Gram-negative rod belongs to Enterobacteriaceae; H. pylori has a distinct curved/spiral morphology and gastric-mucosa niche, unlike the family's straight rod-shaped, GI-tract-colonizing members.

# Item
## label
Organisms outside standard Gram staining

## id
CON-INF-512AD3B7479C2A

## canonical_key
gram-stain.staining-limitations-mycobacteria-mycoplasma-chlamydia

## definition
Some organisms fall outside standard Gram-stain classification: **Mycobacteria** (acid-fast, not Gram-typed), **Mycoplasma** (no cell wall, so cannot Gram-stain), and **Chlamydia** (obligate intracellular, does not reliably Gram-stain).

By contrast, **Neisseria** (Gram-negative diplococci) and **Streptococcus viridans** (Gram-positive cocci) __are reliably visualized by the Gram stain__.

## explicit_objective
Recognize that Mycobacteria, Mycoplasma and Chlamydia are not reliably classified by Gram stain, while Neisseria and Streptococcus viridans are.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids

asu

ASU_Y1=0.25

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
Clinical bacteriology

## subtopic
Staining limitations and acid-fast/spore-forming organisms

## microtopic
Gram-stain exceptions

## nanotopic

## aliases
Gram-stain exceptions
Non-Gram-stainable organisms

## pitfalls
Assuming every clinically relevant bacterium can be classified as Gram-positive or Gram-negative; Mycoplasma lacks a cell wall entirely, Mycobacteria have an atypical mycolic-acid wall requiring acid-fast staining instead, and Chlamydia is an obligate intracellular organism that evades the standard Gram-stain method.

## article_ids
ART-INF-STAINING-LIMITATIONS-SPORE-FORMERS

direct_statement

Also: 7- Which of the following organisms can be stained by gram stain? a) Mycobacteria b) Mycoplasma c) Neisseria d) Chlymedia [printed key: C]

# Item
## label
Staphylococcus: Gram-positive, catalase-positive cocci

## id
CON-INF-101CF957AD4D69

## canonical_key
staphylococcus.genus-identification.catalase-coagulase

## definition
All members of the genus **Staphylococcus** are **Gram-positive cocci** and **catalase-positive**, which distinguishes the genus from *Streptococcus* (catalase-negative).

__Coagulase-positivity is a species-level feature__ that further sets *S. aureus* apart from the other, coagulase-negative, staphylococci.

## explicit_objective
State that Gram-positive cocci plus catalase-positivity are the genus-defining features of Staphylococcus, distinguishing it from Streptococcus.

# Item
## label
S. aureus identification; no endotoxin

## id
CON-INF-B8524FF8069C8E

## canonical_key
staph-aureus.identification-and-virulence

## definition
**Staphylococcus aureus** is **Gram-positive cocci in clusters**, **catalase-positive** and **coagulase-positive**.

Because it is Gram-positive and lacks an outer membrane, it has __no lipopolysaccharide/endotoxin__ — so calling endotoxin a virulence factor of *S. aureus* is incorrect. Its virulence instead relies on **exotoxins, superantigens and antiphagocytic surface proteins**.

## explicit_objective
State that S. aureus's identifying features are Gram-positive cocci, catalase-positive and coagulase-positive, and that endotoxin is NOT one of its virulence factors, since it is a Gram-positive organism.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

## modules
ASU-INF

## universities
asu

## learner_years
1

## blueprint_weight
0.25

## exam_weight_by_year
ASU_Y1=0.25

## clinical_relevance
0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
Clinical bacteriology

## subtopic
Staphylococcus and Streptococcus identification

## microtopic
S. aureus identification and virulence

## nanotopic

## aliases
S. aureus identification
Gram-positive absence of endotoxin

## pitfalls
Assuming every major pathogen relies on endotoxin for virulence; endotoxin (LPS) is exclusive to the Gram-negative outer membrane, so a Gram-positive organism like S. aureus cannot use it -- its virulence instead comes from exotoxins, superantigens (e.g. TSST-1) and antiphagocytic factors (e.g. protein A, capsule).

## article_ids
ART-INF-STAPHYLOCOCCUS-STREPTOCOCCUS-ID

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording
10- Which of the following is incorrect about staph. aureus? a. G+ve cocci in clusters b.Endotoxin is an important virulence factor c. Catalase +ve d. coagulase +ve [printed key: B]

# Item
## label
S. pneumoniae: alpha-hemolytic cause of lobar pneumonia

## id
CON-INF-9A9A3DB5DB40FE

## canonical_key
streptococcus-pneumoniae.identification.alpha-hemolysis

## definition
**Streptococcus pneumoniae** produces **alpha-hemolysis** (partial, greenish hemolysis) on blood agar and appears as **Gram-positive cocci**.

This fits the classic picture of **pneumococcal (lobar) pneumonia** — chest pain, cough and bloody sputum in an older adult.

## explicit_objective
Identify Streptococcus pneumoniae as the alpha-hemolytic, Gram-positive coccus responsible for a classic lobar-pneumonia presentation with bloody sputum.

## concept_type
definition

under review

Streptococcus pneumoniae identification

## pitfalls
Confusing alpha-hemolysis (partial, greenish -- shared by both S. pneumoniae and Streptococcus viridans) with beta-hemolysis (complete, clear, as in S. pyogenes); the clinical picture (older patient, chest pain, bloody sputum) is what points specifically to S. pneumoniae over viridans strep in this presentation.

# Item
## label
All Enterobacteriaceae are facultative anaerobes

## id
CON-INF-819743D002A2DD

## canonical_key
enterobacteriaceae.shared-biochemical-features

## definition
The biochemical feature shared by __every__ member of the family **Enterobacteriaceae** is that they are **facultative anaerobes**, able to grow with or without oxygen.

Other features — universal oxidase-positivity, lactose fermentation, catalase-negativity, or all being true pathogens — are __not__ shared by every member.

## explicit_objective
State that being facultative anaerobes is the biochemical feature common to all members of Enterobacteriaceae, unlike lactose fermentation or oxidase reaction, which vary by genus.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

Enterobacteriaceae and Gram-negative nosocomial pathogens

## microtopic
Enterobacteriaceae shared features

## nanotopic

## aliases
Enterobacteriaceae shared features
Facultative anaerobe family trait

## pitfalls
Assuming every Enterobacteriaceae member ferments lactose (only "lactose fermenters" like E. coli do; Salmonella and Shigella do not) or is oxidase-positive; Enterobacteriaceae are in fact oxidase-NEGATIVE as a family, which is part of what distinguishes them from Pseudomonas.

# Item
## label
Proteus: urease and struvite (staghorn) stones

## id
CON-INF-D07212E157CC7A

## canonical_key
proteus.uti-struvite-stones

## definition
**Proteus** species produce the enzyme **urease**, which hydrolyzes urea to ammonia and __alkalinizes the urine__.

This promotes formation of **struvite (magnesium ammonium phosphate) stones**, making Proteus the classic organism behind a UTI complicated by urinary stones.

## explicit_objective
State that Proteus, via urease production and urine alkalinization, is the organism classically associated with UTI and struvite stone formation.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject

# Item
## label
C. difficile: antibiotic-associated pseudomembranous colitis

## id
CON-INF-74228C21F2257C

## canonical_key
clostridioides-difficile.pseudomembranous-colitis

## definition
**Clostridioides (Clostridium) difficile** is the classic cause of **pseudomembranous colitis**, presenting with bloody diarrhea __after a course of antibiotics__.

Antibiotics disrupt the normal colonic flora, allowing *C. difficile* overgrowth and toxin-mediated colonic damage.

## explicit_objective
Identify Clostridioides difficile as the causative organism of post-antibiotic pseudomembranous colitis.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

## secondary_node_ids

## academic_relevance

0.82

## topic
Clinical bacteriology

## subtopic
Clinical bacteriology case organisms

## microtopic
C. difficile pseudomembranous colitis

## nanotopic

## aliases
C. diff colitis
Antibiotic-associated diarrhea organism

## pitfalls
Attributing post-antibiotic bloody diarrhea to Yersinia enterocolitica, Shigella dysenteriae or Salmonella typhi; those cause diarrhea by direct infection rather than by antibiotic-associated disruption of colonic flora, and none is the classic pseudomembranous-colitis organism.

## article_ids
ART-INF-CLINICAL-BACTERIOLOGY-CASE-ORGANISMS

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording
17- A 33 years old male developed Bloody diarrhea 1 week after being treated with antibiotic course, He was diagnosed with pseudomembranous colitis, causitive organism is: a) Yersinia enterocolitica b) Shigella dysentaria c) Salmonella typhi d) Clostridum difficile [printed key: D]

# Item
## label
Legionella: atypical pneumonia from contaminated water

## id
CON-INF-1AA1761EA50627

## canonical_key
legionella-pneumophila.atypical-pneumonia-water-exposure

## definition
**Legionella pneumophila** causes **atypical pneumonia** classically linked to __inhalation of aerosolized contaminated water__ — from showers, cooling towers or air-conditioning systems.

It presents with chest pain and dry cough after such an exposure.

## explicit_objective
Identify Legionella pneumophila as the organism causing atypical pneumonia after aerosolized contaminated-water exposure.

# Item
## label
P. aeruginosa: oxidase-positive, green pigment

## id
CON-INF-110935663CD77A

## canonical_key
pseudomonas-aeruginosa.identification.oxidase-pigment

## definition
**Pseudomonas aeruginosa** is identified in culture by being **oxidase-positive** and producing a **green pigment (pyocyanin)** on agar — as seen in a ventilated patient with greenish sputum growing Gram-negative bacilli.

These features, with differences in sugar fermentation and oxygen requirement, __distinguish it from Enterobacteriaceae such as E. coli__.

## explicit_objective
Identify Pseudomonas aeruginosa by its oxidase-positive, green-pigment-producing culture characteristics, and distinguish it from E. coli by sugar fermentation, oxidase reaction and oxygen requirement.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

## modules
ASU-INF

## module_subject
ASU-INF > Microbiology > Lectures > Clinical bacteriology (Chapter 6)

## universities
asu

## learner_years
1

## blueprint_weight
0.25

## exam_weight_by_year
ASU_Y1=0.25

## clinical_relevance
0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
Clinical bacteriology

## subtopic
Enterobacteriaceae and Gram-negative nosocomial pathogens

## microtopic
Pseudomonas oxidase and pigment identification

## nanotopic

## aliases
Pseudomonas oxidase test
Pyocyanin pigment

## pitfalls
Assuming oxidase positivity is a shared Gram-negative-rod feature; it specifically distinguishes Pseudomonas (oxidase-positive) from the Enterobacteriaceae (oxidase-negative), alongside differences in sugar fermentation (Pseudomonas is a non-fermenter) and oxygen requirement (Pseudomonas is an obligate aerobe; E. coli is a facultative anaerobe).

## article_ids
ART-INF-ENTEROBACTERIACEAE-GRAM-NEGATIVE-NOSOCOMIAL

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording
19- 37 year old is hospitalized for ventilatory support. After 2 days jhe developed severe cough with greenish sputum. Gram stain shows Gram-ve bacilli & culture grows oxidase +ve bacilli with green discolouration of Agarplate, organism is most likely: a) Sttrept. pneumonae b) Pseudomonas aeruginosa c) Mycoplasma pneumonia d) Legionella pneumophilia [printed key: B]. Also: 20- Which of the following is a diffrence between E. coli and P. aeruginosa a) Sugar fermentation b) Oxidase enzyme c) Oxygen Requirements d) All of the above [printed key: D]

## label
Pasteurella multocida: osteomyelitis after a cat bite

## id
CON-INF-191AE17C2BC242

## canonical_key
pasteurella-multocida.cat-bite-osteomyelitis

## definition
**Pasteurella multocida** is a **Gram-negative coccobacillus** that is part of normal cat (and dog) oral flora.

It is the classic cause of __osteomyelitis or soft-tissue infection following a cat bite__.

## explicit_objective
Identify Pasteurella multocida, a Gram-negative coccobacillus from cat saliva flora, as the causative organism of cat-bite-associated osteomyelitis.

# Item
## label
Bacillus anthracis: transmission by spore inhalation

## id
CON-INF-8C58253847111C

## canonical_key
bacillus-anthracis.transmission.spore-inhalation

## definition
**Bacillus anthracis**, a spore-forming Gram-positive bacillus, is classically transmitted to humans by __inhalation of its highly resistant endospores__, causing inhalational anthrax.

This contrasts with organisms transmitted mainly by vector-borne or fecal-oral routes.

## explicit_objective
Identify Bacillus anthracis as the organism transmitted via inhalation of spores, among vector-borne and fecal-oral alternatives.

## concept_type
definition

inf

Bacillus anthracis transmission

Confusing anthrax's spore-inhalation transmission route with the vector-borne transmission of Yersinia pestis (fleas) or the fecal-oral/foodborne transmission of Yersinia enterocolitica; spore inhalation specifically is the hallmark of B. anthracis among this list.

# Item
## label
S. viridans: endocarditis after a dental procedure

## id
CON-INF-2E4D9F498F4B12

## canonical_key
streptococcus-viridans.infective-endocarditis-dental-procedure

## definition
**Streptococcus viridans**, part of the normal oral flora, is the classic cause of **subacute infective endocarditis** __following a dental procedure__ such as tooth extraction.

The transient bacteremia lets the organism seed a susceptible heart valve.

## explicit_objective
Identify Streptococcus viridans, part of normal oral flora, as the classic cause of infective endocarditis following a dental procedure.

## concept_type
definition

## pitfalls
Attributing post-dental-procedure endocarditis to Staphylococcus aureus, which is more typical of acute endocarditis (e.g. in IV drug users or via a skin source), rather than to the oral-flora organism, Streptococcus viridans, associated with the subacute, dental-procedure-triggered presentation.

# Item
## label
Target alteration (30S) resists aminoglycosides

## id
CON-INF-D12D329489668C

## canonical_key
antibiotic-resistance.mechanism.target-alteration-30s

## definition
One mechanism of antibiotic resistance is **target-site alteration**.

A mutation changing the structure of the bacterial ribosomal **30S subunit** __prevents an aminoglycoside from binding__, conferring resistance specifically to that drug class.

## explicit_objective
State that alteration of the ribosomal 30S subunit is a resistance mechanism specific to aminoglycosides.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

inf

Antibiotic resistance

Antibiotic resistance mechanisms

Target-site alteration

## nanotopic

30S ribosomal target alteration
Aminoglycoside resistance mechanism

## pitfalls
Confusing 30S-subunit alteration (aminoglycoside resistance) with other ribosomal or cell-wall target changes conferring resistance to unrelated drug classes such as cephalosporins, quinolones, or sulfa drugs.

# Item
## label
Altered permeability resists tetracycline and amikacin

## id
CON-INF-DFC3D949513ED2

## canonical_key
antibiotic-resistance.mechanism.altered-permeability

## definition
A change in **bacterial membrane permeability** that reduces antibiotic entry into the cell is the resistance mechanism __specifically linked to tetracycline and amikacin__ among the drug pairs tested in this module.

## explicit_objective
State that altered drug permeability is the resistance mechanism specific to tetracycline and amikacin.

definition

Mixing up which drug pair goes with which resistance mechanism; permeability change is specifically linked to tetracycline/amikacin here, not to the penicillin/chloramphenicol, streptomycin/quinolone, or rifampicin/sulfonamide pairings also listed as distractors.

# Item
## label
Acquired vs intrinsic genetic resistance

## id
CON-INF-DCD82D2A1D396C

## canonical_key
antibiotic-resistance.acquired-vs-intrinsic-genetic

## definition
**Acquired genetic resistance** is gained through a new resistance gene — for example **penicillin resistance in Staphylococcus aureus** via **beta-lactamase**.

**Intrinsic (inherent) resistance** is exemplified by **Mycoplasma**, naturally resistant to cell-wall-active antibiotics because it has __no cell wall to target__ in the first place.

## explicit_objective
Distinguish acquired genetic resistance (e.g. S. aureus penicillin resistance via a gained gene) from intrinsic/inherent genetic resistance (e.g. Mycoplasma's natural resistance to cell-wall antibiotics).

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

## modules
ASU-INF

## module_subject
ASU-INF > Microbiology > Lectures > Antibiotic resistance (Chapter 7)

## universities
asu

## learner_years
1

## blueprint_weight
0.25

## exam_weight_by_year
ASU_Y1=0.25

## clinical_relevance
0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
Antibiotic resistance

## subtopic
Antibiotic resistance mechanisms

## microtopic
Acquired vs intrinsic resistance

## nanotopic

## aliases
Acquired vs intrinsic resistance
Inherent genetic resistance

## pitfalls
Assuming any genetically based resistance is "acquired"; Mycoplasma's resistance to cell-wall antibiotics is intrinsic/inherent (it never had the target structure to begin with), not gained through a new resistance gene the way S. aureus penicillin resistance is.

## article_ids
ART-INF-ANTIBIOTIC-RESISTANCE-MECHANISMS

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording
4- Which of the following is an example for acquired genetic resistances a. Vancomycin resistance by E.coli b. Ampicillin resistance by klebsiella c. Vancomycin resistance by klebsiella d. Penicillin resistance by staphylococcus aureus [printed key: D]. Also: 5- Mycoplasma resistance to Cell wall antibiotics is an example for: a) Non genetic resistance b) inherit genetic resistance c) aquired genetic resistance d) mutations [printed key: B]

# Item
## label
R-plasmids carry antibiotic-resistance genes

## id
CON-INF-134BE2C9B827D5

## canonical_key
antibiotic-resistance.mobile-genetic-elements.r-plasmid

## definition
Among mobile genetic elements, the **R-plasmid (resistance plasmid)** is the vehicle that __carries genes for bacterial antibiotic resistance__.

This is distinct from a temperate phage, a lytic phage, or a virulence plasmid.

## explicit_objective
Identify the R-plasmid as the mobile genetic element that carries genes for bacterial antibiotic resistance.

definition

# Item
## label
Beta-lactamase inactivates the drug

## id
CON-INF-64A7823DCEC6E5

## canonical_key
antibiotic-resistance.mechanism.beta-lactamase-inactivation

## definition
**Beta-lactamase** confers resistance by __directly hydrolyzing and inactivating the beta-lactam antibiotic__.

This is distinct from altering drug permeability, pumping the drug out of the cell, or preventing beta-lactam entry.

## explicit_objective
State that beta-lactamase causes resistance specifically by inactivating (hydrolyzing) the beta-lactam drug, not by altering permeability or blocking entry.

## concept_type
definition

under review

Beta-lactamase mechanism

Beta-lactamase inactivation
Beta-lactam hydrolysis

## pitfalls
Confusing enzymatic drug inactivation (beta-lactamase's actual mechanism) with permeability-based mechanisms (altered porins, efflux pumps, or blocked entry), which are separate resistance strategies used by other mechanisms.

antibiotic-resistance.plasmid-vs-chromosome-mediated

## definition
**Plasmid-mediated** resistance mechanisms in this module include **enzyme inactivation** of the drug (e.g. beta-lactamase), **active efflux pumps** and **porin/permeability modification**.

**Chromosome-mutation-mediated** mechanisms include __alteration of the 30S ribosomal subunit__ and __alteration of DNA gyrase__ — not the efflux pumps, which this module groups with the plasmid-mediated mechanisms.

## explicit_objective
Distinguish which resistance mechanisms are grouped as plasmid-mediated (enzyme inactivation, efflux pumps, porin modification) versus chromosome-mutation-mediated (30S subunit alteration, DNA gyrase alteration) in this module.

definition

Chromosome-mutation-mediated resistance

## pitfalls
Assuming ribosomal target alteration (30S subunit) is plasmid-mediated; in this module's framework it is grouped with chromosome-mutation-mediated mechanisms instead, alongside DNA gyrase alteration, while enzyme inactivation and efflux/porin changes are grouped as plasmid-mediated.

# Item
## label
Review antibiotic duration at 72 hours

## id
CON-INF-1872C1B986AAD3

## canonical_key
antibiotic-stewardship.treatment-duration-review

## definition
As an **antibiotic stewardship** principle, the duration and appropriateness of a treatment course is __reviewed after 72 hours__.

This allows reassessment based on clinical response and culture results, rather than continuing indefinitely on the initial empiric choice.

## explicit_objective

# Item
## label
What selects for resistant mutants

## id
CON-INF-7C50CF18CD73F7

## canonical_key
antibiotic-stewardship.resistance-selection-factors

## definition
Selection of antibiotic-resistant mutants is enhanced by a **wrong choice** of antibiotic, an **interrupted course**, and **use when not indicated** — all of which expose bacteria to __sub-lethal drug pressure__.

An adequately **high dose** (as part of correct dosing) is __not__ itself a factor that enhances resistance selection.

## explicit_objective
Identify wrong antibiotic choice, interrupted courses, and unindicated antibiotic use as factors that enhance selection of resistant mutants, and recognize that an adequately high (correct) dose is not one of these factors.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
