Antibiotic resistance

## subtopic
Antibiotic stewardship

## microtopic
Resistance-selection factors

## nanotopic

## aliases
Resistance-selection factors
Antibiotic misuse and resistance

## pitfalls
Assuming a high antibiotic dose promotes resistance; in this module's stewardship framework, it is UNDER-dosing, interrupted courses, wrong drug choice, and unindicated use that enhance resistance selection by exposing bacteria to sub-lethal pressure -- an adequately high dose does the opposite.

ART-INF-ANTIBIOTIC-STEWARDSHIP

# Item
## label
Molds have a filamentous morphology

## id
CON-INF-8D2EDDBECE528D

## canonical_key
fungi.morphology.molds-filamentous

## definition
**Molds** — one of the two basic fungal growth forms — have a __filamentous morphology__, consisting of thread-like **hyphae**.

This contrasts with **yeasts** (unicellular, budding) and **dimorphic fungi** (able to switch between the two forms).

## explicit_objective
State that filamentous growth is the defining morphology of molds.

# Item
## label
Fungal cell wall: glucan and mannan

## id
CON-INF-37160A7BC0B002

## canonical_key
fungi.cell-wall.glucan-mannan-composition

## definition
A characterizing feature of medically important fungi is a cell wall composed of **glucan and mannan** (polysaccharide/glycoprotein components).

This __distinguishes fungi from bacteria__ (peptidoglycan wall) and from organisms whose membrane sterol is cholesterol rather than **ergosterol**.

## explicit_objective
State that the cell wall of medically important fungi is composed of glucan and mannan.

## concept_type
definition

# Item
## label
Spore-forming bacilli is not a fungal feature

## id
CON-INF-5FD079DAC1BCFB

## canonical_key
fungi.morphology.excludes-bacterial-spore-forming

## definition
True fungal morphological features include being **dimorphic**, having **non-septate hyphae**, and existing as **unicellular yeast** cells.

"**Spore-forming bacilli**" describes a __bacterial, not fungal, morphology__ and is not a feature of fungi.

## explicit_objective
Identify "spore-forming bacilli" as a bacterial morphological description that does not apply to fungi, distinguishing it from true fungal morphological features (dimorphism, non-septate hyphae, unicellular yeast forms).

# Item
## label
Dimorphic fungi: mold at 25C, yeast at 37C

## id
CON-INF-44400FF4328CA8

## canonical_key
fungi.dimorphism.mold-vs-yeast-form

## definition
**Dimorphic fungi** form **hyphae (mold)** in nature at **25C** (room temperature) and convert to a **yeast** form at **37C** (body temperature, in vivo).

This __temperature-dependent switch__ underlies their ability to cause both cutaneous and systemic infection.

## explicit_objective
State that dimorphic fungi grow as a mold in nature (25C) and as a yeast in vivo (37C), and that this switch underlies their capacity for both cutaneous and systemic disease.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

inf

Mycology, virology and infection control

## subtopic
Fungal morphology and cell wall

## microtopic
Fungal dimorphism

## nanotopic

Mold-to-yeast temperature switch

## pitfalls
Describing dimorphism only by its clinical consequence (cutaneous & systemic infection capability) rather than by its defining temperature-dependent mold/yeast switch, which is the more precise and complete description this module credits as "best."

# Item
## label
Fungal spores are reproductive, not survival, structures

## id
CON-INF-2FEBA5328F9E83

## canonical_key
fungi.spores.reproductive-vs-survival-role

## definition
**Fungal spores** are an __essential, routine part of the fungal life cycle__, used for reproduction and dispersal.

This differs from **bacterial spores**, which are produced only under adverse survival conditions. Fungal spores are not restricted to filamentous fungi alone, nor universal to every species.

## explicit_objective
State that fungal spores are an essential part of the normal fungal life cycle, distinguishing them from bacterial endospores, which form specifically as a survival response to adverse conditions.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

Fungal reproductive spores
Fungal vs bacterial spore role

## pitfalls
Assuming fungal spores serve the same survival-under-stress role as bacterial endospores; fungal spores are instead a routine reproductive structure produced as part of the normal fungal life cycle, not only under adverse conditions.

# Item
## label
Chitin is the main fungal wall component

## id
CON-INF-5711F66EFF586F

## canonical_key
fungi.cell-wall.chitin-main-component

## definition
Most fungal cell walls are composed mostly of **chitin**, a structural polysaccharide.

It is __not__ peptidoglycan (bacterial), cellulose, or lipopolysaccharide — chitin is the testable answer for the main component of the fungal wall.

## explicit_objective
State that chitin is the main component of most fungal cell walls.

## concept_type
definition

# Item
## label
Aggregated hyphae form the mycelium

## id
CON-INF-7213E96DAD38D1

## canonical_key
fungi.morphology.hyphae-mycelium

## definition
When individual fungal **hyphae** aggregate into a visible mass, the resulting macroscopic structure is called the **mycelium**.

It is distinct from a yeast form, a hyphal body, or fungal spores.

## explicit_objective

# Item
## label
KOH prep visualizes fungi by dissolving keratin

## id
CON-INF-09F0BCC572E758

## canonical_key
fungi.laboratory-diagnosis.koh-preparation

## definition
The **potassium hydroxide (KOH) preparation** is used to visualize fungal elements in skin scrapings or nail clippings.

It works by __dissolving host keratin__ so that fungal hyphae and spores become visible under the microscope — distinct from India ink prep, Gram staining, or PAS staining.

## explicit_objective
State that KOH preparation visualizes fungal elements in skin/nail specimens by dissolving keratin.

## concept_type
definition

Keratin-dissolving fungal stain

Confusing KOH preparation (dissolves keratin for direct fungal visualization) with India ink (used for encapsulated organisms like Cryptococcus), Gram stain (bacterial), or PAS stain (a different fungal/tissue stain not based on keratin dissolution).

# Item
## label
Polyenes bind ergosterol and form membrane pores

## id
CON-INF-15A9CB1D102D73

## canonical_key
antifungal.polyenes.ergosterol-binding-mechanism

## definition
**Polyene** antifungals such as **amphotericin B** act by __binding ergosterol in the fungal cell membrane and forming pores__, disrupting membrane integrity.

This is distinct from blocking microtubule assembly, inhibiting nucleic acid synthesis, or inhibiting beta-glucan synthesis.

## explicit_objective
State that polyenes act by binding ergosterol and forming membrane pores.

## concept_type
definition

under review

# Item
## label
Echinocandins inhibit beta-glucan synthase

## id
CON-INF-8C46B66176DADA

## canonical_key
antifungal.echinocandins.beta-glucan-synthase-inhibition

## definition
**Echinocandins** (e.g. **caspofungin**) act by __inhibiting beta-glucan synthase__, the enzyme that produces beta-glucan — a key structural component of the fungal cell wall.

This differs from ergosterol synthesis inhibition (azoles), microtubule formation, or capsule synthesis.

## explicit_objective
State that echinocandins act by inhibiting beta-glucan synthase, disrupting fungal cell wall synthesis.

definition

# Item
## label
The capsid is present in all viruses

## id
CON-INF-5544B0DB9FAF82

## canonical_key
virus.structure.capsid-universal-component

## definition
The **capsid** — the protein coat surrounding the viral genome, together forming the **nucleocapsid** — is the one structural component found in __all viruses__, enveloped or not, unlike an envelope, spikes, or a fixed nucleic acid type.

Its functions are protecting the genome, mediating attachment to host-cell receptors, and conferring antigenicity. __It does not confer sensitivity to 70% alcohol__ — that property belongs to the lipid envelope.

## explicit_objective
State that the capsid (nucleocapsid) is the structural component found in all viruses, and list its true functions (genome protection, attachment, antigenicity) versus one that is not its function (alcohol sensitivity).

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

Universal viral capsid

## pitfalls
Assuming envelope is present in all viruses (many viruses are non-enveloped, but ALL viruses have a capsid); assuming the capsid confers alcohol sensitivity, which is instead a property of the lipid envelope, absent in non-enveloped viruses.

Sensitivity to 70% alcohol [printed key: D]. Also: 32- Which of the following components is found in all viruses? A) Mitochondria B) Ribosomes C) Nucleo-capsid D) Cell wall [printed key: C]

# Item
## label
A viroid is a naked circular RNA

## id
CON-INF-6EE705523FD61C

## canonical_key
virus.viroid.definition

## definition
A **viroid** is a small, **circular RNA** molecule __lacking both an envelope and a protein coat (capsid)__.

It is distinct from a **satellite virus** (needs a helper virus to replicate) and a **prion** (an infectious protein without nucleic acid).

## explicit_objective
State that a viroid is a circular RNA molecule without an envelope or coat.

## concept_type
definition

# Item
## label
Enveloped virion = nucleocapsid plus envelope

## id
CON-INF-9C0D5FBED79267

## canonical_key
virus.structure.enveloped-virion-composition

## definition
The complete virus particle (**virion**) of an enveloped virus is the **nucleocapsid** (genome plus capsid) __surrounded by an envelope__.

It is not the nucleocapsid alone, nor the imprecisely worded "core plus envelope" or "coat plus envelope."

## explicit_objective
State that the virion of an enveloped virus is composed of nucleocapsid plus envelope.

# Item
## label
The viral envelope: host-derived lipid, viral protein

## id
CON-INF-7952070C4BD8AF

## canonical_key
virus.envelope.origin-and-composition

## definition
The **viral envelope** is **lipoprotein** in nature. Its __lipid is acquired from the host cell's plasma or nuclear membrane during budding__, while its protein is coded by the **viral** genome (not the host genome).

It is **sensitive to detergents**, which dissolve its lipid component.

## explicit_objective
State that the viral envelope's lipid is host-derived (via budding) while its protein is viral-genome-coded, and that the envelope is detergent-sensitive.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## pitfalls
Assuming the envelope's protein component is coded by the host genome; it is coded by the VIRAL genome even though the lipid bilayer itself is host-derived -- mixing up which envelope component comes from which genome is the key trap here.

# Item
## label
General viral properties; phenol is not universally lethal

## id
CON-INF-1165F8B9564697

## canonical_key
virus.general-properties.destroyed-by-phenol-exception

## definition
Viruses have **either DNA or RNA** (never both as their genome), are **non-susceptible to antibiotics**, and are **obligate intracellular** organisms.

Being "destroyed by phenol" is __not__ a universal viral property — **non-enveloped (naked) viruses** are notably more resistant to phenol and similar disinfectants than enveloped viruses.

## explicit_objective
List the general properties shared by all viruses (single nucleic acid type, antibiotic non-susceptibility, obligate intracellular existence), and recognize that reliable destruction by phenol is not one of them.

## concept_type
definition

# Item
## label
Latent viral infection: an occult, hidden form

## id
CON-INF-4EC402ABB55946

## canonical_key
virus.infection-patterns.latent

## definition
A **latent** viral infection is one in which the virus persists in an __occult (hidden, non-productive) form__ inside the infected cell.

This differs from a **chronic** infection (ongoing, detectable low-level replication) and a **slow** infection (long incubation, progressive disease). A virus can persist after recovery from acute illness through any of these mechanisms.

## explicit_objective
Define latent viral infection as an occult, non-productive form of the virus persisting inside the infected cell, and recognize that viral persistence after recovery can occur via chronic, latent, or slow infection.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

inf

Latent viral infection

## pitfalls
Confusing latent infection (occult, non-productive persistence) with chronic infection (ongoing, detectable low-level viral replication) or slow infection (a long incubation period followed by progressive disease, as in prion diseases or SSPE) -- all three are distinct mechanisms of post-recovery viral persistence.

# Item
## label
Systemic vs localized viral infection

## id
CON-INF-46988C6D071F21

## canonical_key
virus.infection-patterns.systemic-vs-localized

## definition
A **systemic** viral infection is characterized by **viremia**, involvement of **multiple organs**, and (typically) __long-lasting immunity__ — not by a short incubation period.

A **localized** infection multiplies at the entry site, has a **short incubation period**, produces no long-lasting immunity, and does not involve severe viremia.

## explicit_objective
Distinguish systemic viral infection (viremia, multi-organ spread, longer incubation, long-lasting immunity) from localized viral infection (multiplication at entry site, short incubation, no long-lasting immunity, no severe viremia).

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

## modules
ASU-INF

## module_subject
ASU-INF > Microbiology > Lectures > Mycology, virology and infection control (Chapters 8-10)

# Item
## label
Rabies virus produces Negri bodies

## id
CON-INF-8989679385BD3C

## canonical_key
rabies-virus.negri-bodies

## definition
Among viral causes of inclusion bodies, **rabies virus** is the one associated with **Negri inclusion bodies** — cytoplasmic inclusions found in __infected neurons__.

These are distinct from the inclusion bodies of measles, herpes, or pox viruses.

## explicit_objective
Identify rabies virus as the cause of Negri inclusion bodies.

definition

# Item
## label
What viral inclusion bodies are

## id
CON-INF-514E4CAEB62A82

## canonical_key
virus.inclusion-bodies.general-properties

## definition
**Viral inclusion bodies** can be aggregates of virions, aggregates of viral proteins, or degenerative changes in an infected cell.

They are __not detectable only by electron microscopy__ — many classic inclusion bodies, such as **Negri bodies**, are visible by routine light microscopy with appropriate staining.

## explicit_objective
List the true properties of viral inclusion bodies (aggregates of virions/proteins, degenerative cell changes) and recognize that detection is not limited to electron microscopy.

## concept_type
definition

# Item
## label
Hemadsorption detects viral growth in culture

## id
CON-INF-36861264D145AB

## canonical_key
virus.culture-detection.hemadsorption

## definition
**Hemadsorption** detects viral growth on tissue culture: red blood cells added to the culture __adhere to infected cells that display viral hemagglutinin__ on their surface, indicating replication has occurred.

It is one of several ways to identify viral growth, alongside cell lysis and the interference phenomenon.

## explicit_objective
State that hemadsorption detects viral growth via infected cells adsorbing RBCs, and recognize that cell lysis, interference and hemadsorption together are all valid ways to recognize viral growth on tissue culture.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids

## subtopic
Viral culture detection, entry and replication

## microtopic
Hemadsorption

## nanotopic

## aliases
Hemadsorption test
RBC adsorption by infected cells

## pitfalls
Confusing hemadsorption (infected cells adsorb RBCs onto their surface, indicating hemagglutinin expression) with hemagglutination (a cell-free reaction between virus particles and RBCs) or with hemolysis (RBC destruction) -- hemadsorption specifically means RBCs sticking to the infected culture cells themselves.

## article_ids
ART-INF-VIRAL-CULTURE-DETECTION-ENTRY-REPLICATION

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording
17- RBCs can be used for detection of viral growth on tissue culture by a. Virally infected cells adsorb RBCs b. Virus kills RBCs c. Virus interferes with replication of another virus inside RBCs d. Virus causes rounding of RBCs e. Virus inhibits agglutination of RBCs [printed key: A]. Also: 25- Viral growth on tissue culture can be recognized by: a. Cell lysis b. Interference phenomenon c. Haemadsorption d. All of the above [printed key: D]

# Item
## label
Viral entry: endocytosis or envelope fusion

## id
CON-INF-F595E6CEC3AFD2

## canonical_key
virus.entry.fusion-vs-endocytosis

## definition
The **entry and penetration** step — following attachment — occurs by **endocytosis** or **fusion of the viral envelope** with the host cell membrane.

These differ from simple receptor interaction (which mediates **attachment**, a prior step) and from budding/exocytosis (which mediate viral **release**, not entry).

## explicit_objective
State that viral entry/penetration occurs via endocytosis or envelope-membrane fusion, distinguishing this step from attachment (receptor interaction) and from release (budding/exocytosis).

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

Mycology, virology and infection control

## subtopic
Viral culture detection, entry and replication

## microtopic
Viral entry mechanisms

## nanotopic

## aliases
Viral entry mechanism
Endocytosis and membrane fusion

## pitfalls
Confusing entry/penetration (endocytosis or fusion) with the earlier attachment step (receptor interaction alone, without membrane crossing) or with budding/exocytosis, which describe the opposite process of viral release from the cell.

## article_ids
ART-INF-VIRAL-CULTURE-DETECTION-ENTRY-REPLICATION

direct_statement

29- Entry and penetration of virus through host cell membrane occurs by: a. Interaction between the virus & specific host cell receptors b. Endocytosis or fusion of the virus envelope with host cell membrane c. Budding d. Exocytosis [printed key: B]

# Item
## label
Envelope glycoproteins, the capsid, and specific host-cell receptors contribute to viral attachment, but lysozymes do not

## id
CON-INF-62046515A155BD

## canonical_key
virus.entry.attachment-factors

## definition
Viral attachment to the host cell is contributed to by envelope glycoproteins, the capsid, and specific host-cell receptors; lysozymes -- a host antimicrobial enzyme that degrades bacterial peptidoglycan -- play no role in viral attachment.

## explicit_objective
List the factors that contribute to viral attachment (envelope glycoproteins, capsid, specific host receptors) and recognize that lysozymes do not.

definition

# Item
## label
The viral replication cycle proceeds: attachment, penetration, uncoating, replication, assembly, release

## id
CON-INF-2C32D6BA60368C

## canonical_key
virus.replication-cycle.sequence

## definition
The correct sequence of the virus replication cycle is: attachment (to the host cell), penetration (entry into the cell), uncoating (release of the viral genome from the capsid), replication (of the viral genome and synthesis of viral components), assembly (of new virions), and release (from the host cell).

## explicit_objective
State the correct order of the viral replication cycle: attachment, penetration, uncoating, replication, assembly, release.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

ASU_Y1=0.25

0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

0.82

## topic
Mycology, virology and infection control

## subtopic
Viral culture detection, entry and replication

## microtopic
Viral replication cycle sequence

## nanotopic

## aliases
Viral replication cycle
Attachment-penetration-uncoating-replication-assembly-release

## pitfalls
Placing uncoating after replication (the genome must be uncoated/released from the capsid BEFORE it can be replicated and expressed) or placing assembly before replication (new virion components cannot be assembled until the genome has been replicated and viral proteins synthesized).

## article_ids
ART-INF-VIRAL-CULTURE-DETECTION-ENTRY-REPLICATION

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording
36- Which of the following is the correct sequence of virus replication cycle? a. Attachment penetration, replication, assembly, uncoating, release. b. Attachment penetration, replication, uncoating, assembly, release c. Attachment, penetration, uncoating, replication, assembly, release d. Attachment, uncoating, penetration, assembly, replication, release e. Uncoating, attachment, penetration, replication, assembly, release [printed key: C]

# Item
## label
A negative-sense ssRNA virus genome must be transcribed by RNA polymerase and cannot itself act as mRNA

## id
CON-INF-20755F48E062B0

## canonical_key
virus.genome.negative-sense-ssrna-properties

## definition
A negative-sense single-stranded RNA (-ssRNA) virus carries a genome that must be transcribed by an RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (carried in the virion) before it can be translated, because the genome strand itself is single-stranded and cannot directly act as mRNA and is NOT itself infectious on its own -- the infectious unit requires the accompanying polymerase.

## explicit_objective
State that a -ve sense ssRNA genome must be transcribed by RNA polymerase before translation, cannot itself act as mRNA, and is not infectious as a naked strand on its own.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

## universities
asu

0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
Mycology, virology and infection control

## subtopic
Viral culture detection, entry and replication

## microtopic
Negative-sense ssRNA virus properties

## nanotopic

## aliases
Negative-sense RNA virus
-ve sense ssRNA properties

## pitfalls
Assuming a -ve sense ssRNA strand is directly infectious on its own (like a +ve sense ssRNA genome, which CAN act as mRNA and is infectious by itself); a -ve sense genome requires its own RNA-dependent RNA polymerase to first make a complementary, translatable mRNA copy.

## article_ids
ART-INF-VIRAL-CULTURE-DETECTION-ENTRY-REPLICATION

## support_mode
direct_statement

35- All of the following is true regarding -ve sense ssRNA virus except a. It must be transcribed by RNA polymerase b. It cann't act as mRNA c. -ve sense ssRNA strand is infectious d. genome is Single Stranded. [printed key: C]

# Item
## label
Oncogenic viruses transform cells by inserting a viral oncogene, mutating a regulatory gene, or inactivating a suppressor gene -- not by a viral-coded toxin

## id
CON-INF-2AFE43527FBBCC

## canonical_key
virus.oncogenesis.transformation-mechanisms

## definition
Mechanisms of cell transformation by oncogenic viruses include insertion of a viral oncogene, mutation of a host regulatory gene, and production of a viral protein that inactivates a cell suppressor gene; production of a viral-coded toxin is NOT a recognized mechanism of oncogenic transformation.

## explicit_objective
List the mechanisms of oncogenic viral cell transformation (oncogene insertion, regulatory gene mutation, suppressor gene inactivation) and recognize that a viral-coded toxin is not one of them.

## concept_type
definition

## pitfalls

# Item
## label
Cell tropism is determined by specific virus receptors, proteolytic enzyme availability, and favorable temperature/pH, but not by absence of immunity

## id
CON-INF-CE64E7CD7B4393

## canonical_key
virus.cell-tropism.determinants

## definition
The mechanism of viral "cell tropism" -- why a virus infects some cell types/tissues and not others -- is determined by presence of a specific virus receptor on the cell, availability of a proteolytic enzyme the virus requires, and favorable temperature and pH conditions in that cell/tissue; absence of immunity against the virus in those sites is NOT a mechanism of cell tropism, since tropism is a property of the virus-cell interaction itself, not of local immune status.

## explicit_objective
List the true determinants of viral cell tropism (specific receptor, proteolytic enzyme, favorable temperature/pH) and recognize that absence of local immunity is not one of them.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

## universities

ASU_Y1=0.25

0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

0.82

## topic
Mycology, virology and infection control

## subtopic
Viral oncogenesis, tropism and retroviral integration

## microtopic
Cell tropism determinants

## nanotopic

## aliases
Viral cell tropism
Tropism determinants

## pitfalls
Confusing cell tropism (a property of the virus-cell molecular interaction: receptor, enzyme, temperature/pH) with immune evasion or immune status of a tissue, which is a separate concept from why a virus can or cannot infect a given cell type in the first place.

## article_ids
ART-INF-VIRAL-ONCOGENESIS-TROPISM-RETROVIRAL-INTEGRATION

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording
28- Mechanism of "Cell tropism" include all of the following except a. presence of Specific virus receptor on the cell b. Presence of proteolytic enzyme required by the virus c. presence favourable Temperature & pH of the cell d. Absence of immunity against virus in those sites [printed key: D]

# Item
## label
Integrase is the viral component crucial for integration of retroviral DNA into the host genome

## id
CON-INF-A8824D935972A1

## canonical_key
retrovirus.integrase.genome-integration

## definition
Among retroviral components, integrase is specifically the enzyme crucial for integrating the reverse-transcribed retroviral DNA into the host cell genome -- distinct from reverse transcriptase (which synthesizes the DNA copy from the RNA genome), protease (which cleaves viral polyproteins), or RNA polymerase.

## explicit_objective
Identify integrase as the retroviral enzyme responsible for integrating retroviral DNA into the host genome, distinct from reverse transcriptase and protease.

## concept_type
definition

under review

## microtopic
Retroviral integrase

## nanotopic

Retroviral integrase

## pitfalls
Confusing integrase (integrates viral DNA into the host genome) with reverse transcriptase (makes the DNA copy from RNA, an earlier step) or protease (cleaves viral polyproteins during maturation, a later step) -- each retroviral enzyme has a distinct, sequential role.

# Item
## label
Reporting a sharps injury includes PEP administration, exposure assessment and HCP follow-up, but not preventing the HCP from working

## id
CON-INF-E8E1DEE578851C

## canonical_key
infection-control.sharps-injury.reporting-and-pep

## definition
Indications for reporting a sharps injury incident in healthcare include administration of post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP), allowing follow-up for the exposed healthcare provider (HCP), and assessment of the type and severity of exposure; preventing the exposed HCP from working is NOT among these indications -- the exposed worker is not automatically removed from duty simply because a sharps injury occurred and is being reported/managed.

## explicit_objective
List the true indications for reporting a sharps injury (PEP administration, HCP follow-up, exposure assessment) and recognize that preventing the exposed HCP from working is not one of them.

definition

# Item
## label
Abdominal examination has the lowest risk of transmitting infection during healthcare delivery among common procedures

## id
CON-INF-1667BEB0F19C54

## canonical_key
infection-control.procedure-risk-stratification

## definition
Among common healthcare procedures, abdominal examination carries the lowest risk of acquiring infection during healthcare delivery, in contrast to invasive procedures such as intravenous injection, placement of urinary catheters, and surgical procedures, all of which breach the skin/mucosal barrier and carry substantially higher infection risk.

infection-control.protective-environment-precautions

## definition
Protective environment precautions -- a room with POSITIVE-pressure ventilation, designed to protect a highly vulnerable patient from organisms in the surrounding air -- are recommended for allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) patients and, more broadly, for other highly immunocompromised patients, distinguishing this precaution category from contact, droplet, and airborne precautions (which use negative pressure and protect others FROM the patient, the opposite direction of protection).

## explicit_objective
State that protective environment precautions (positive-pressure rooms) are used for allogeneic HSCT and other highly immunocompromised patients, and recognize the opposite direction of protection compared to airborne/droplet/contact precautions.

definition

# Item
## label
Heavy duty gloves should be worn for cleaning contaminated instruments, handling waste, and cleaning contaminated surfaces

## id
CON-INF-F00BFB8CD11CD5

## canonical_key
infection-control.ppe.glove-selection-by-task

## definition
For tasks such as cleaning contaminated instruments, handling waste, and cleaning contaminated surfaces, heavy-duty gloves are the appropriate PPE choice -- offering greater puncture/chemical resistance than surgical gloves, clean latex gloves, or transparent gloves, which are used for other clinical tasks.

# Item
## label
Hand hygiene, patient skin antisepsis, no-touch technique and sterile gloves are aseptic technique components; a high-efficiency mask is not

## id
CON-INF-5315A4F1C792BC

## canonical_key
infection-control.aseptic-technique.components

## definition
Aseptic technique includes hand hygiene with antiseptic soap, patient skin antisepsis, no-touch technique, and wearing sterile gloves; wearing a high-efficiency (N95) mask is NOT a component of aseptic technique -- that mask type belongs instead to airborne-precaution PPE for protecting the wearer from inhaling infectious airborne particles, a different infection-control category from maintaining a sterile field.

## explicit_objective
List the components of aseptic technique (hand hygiene, skin antisepsis, no-touch technique, sterile gloves) and recognize that a high-efficiency mask belongs to airborne precautions, not to aseptic technique.

# Item
## label
Endoscopes are disinfected after cleaning, unlike items belonging to sterile-tissue-contact categories

## id
CON-INF-1013FCDCBC8C6E

## canonical_key
infection-control.reprocessing.endoscope-risk-category

## definition
Endoscopes are correctly described as being disinfected after cleaning (high-level disinfection), reflecting their status as semi-critical items that contact mucous membranes but not normally sterile tissue -- they do NOT belong to the same reprocessing category as vascular catheters or intrauterine devices (critical items requiring sterilization, since those contact sterile tissue or the bloodstream directly).

## explicit_objective
State that endoscopes require high-level disinfection after cleaning (semi-critical items), distinguishing their reprocessing category from critical items like vascular catheters or intrauterine devices, which require sterilization.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## pitfalls
Grouping endoscopes with critical items like vascular catheters or intrauterine devices, which require full sterilization because they contact sterile tissue or the bloodstream; endoscopes are semi-critical (mucous-membrane contact) and require high-level disinfection, not full sterilization.

# Item
## label
Airborne precautions require HCW vaccination (where available), an N95 mask, single-room isolation, and NEGATIVE (not positive) air pressure

## id
CON-INF-92B5DD983CF7D8

## canonical_key
infection-control.airborne-precautions.requirements

## definition
Airborne precautions include, where applicable, vaccination for the healthcare worker (HCW), a high-efficiency (N95) mask for the HCW, and isolation of the patient in a single room; they specifically require NEGATIVE-pressure ventilation, not positive-pressure -- positive-pressure ventilation belongs instead to protective environment precautions -- and a surgical mask for the patient is generally not the standard requirement (the HCW's N95 mask, and single-room isolation, are the key elements, alongside a source-control mask on the patient if needed during transport).

## explicit_objective
List the true components of airborne precautions (HCW vaccination where available, N95 mask, single-room isolation) and identify that positive-pressure ventilation is NOT included -- airborne precautions use negative pressure, the opposite of protective environment precautions.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## pitfalls

# Item
## label
An unvaccinated HCW exposed to HBV should receive 1 dose of vaccine plus 1 dose of HBIg

## id
CON-INF-27F27DE6B69D41

## canonical_key
infection-control.hepatitis-b.post-exposure-prophylaxis

## definition
The appropriate post-exposure prophylaxis for an unvaccinated healthcare worker exposed to hepatitis B virus (HBV) is to initiate 1 dose of hepatitis B vaccine PLUS 1 dose of hepatitis B immune globulin (HBIg) -- combining active immunization (vaccine) with immediate passive protection (HBIg) -- rather than either component alone or antiviral chemotherapy.

## explicit_objective
State that post-exposure prophylaxis for an unvaccinated HCW exposed to HBV combines 1 dose of vaccine with 1 dose of HBIg.

definition

## pitfalls
Giving only the vaccine (provides delayed, active immunity too slow to prevent infection from this specific exposure) or only HBIg (provides only immediate passive protection without building long-term immunity) instead of the combined vaccine+HBIg approach for an unvaccinated exposed HCW.

# Item
## label
Proper disposal of used needles means placing the needle and syringe in a puncture-resistant container

## id
CON-INF-D66B8139A1AA02

## canonical_key
infection-control.sharps-disposal.safe-practice

## definition
Proper disposal of used needles requires placing the needle and its attached syringe directly into a puncture-resistant sharps container -- NOT breaking or bending the needle, NOT recapping it, and NOT separating the needle from the syringe first, all of which increase the risk of an accidental needlestick injury.

## explicit_objective
State that proper needle disposal means placing the needle and syringe intact into a puncture-resistant container, and recognize that breaking, recapping, or separating the needle are unsafe practices.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

DIS-MIC

## topic
Mycology, virology and infection control

## subtopic
Occupational exposure and sharps safety

## microtopic
Sharps disposal safe practice

## nanotopic

## aliases
Sharps disposal
Puncture-resistant container

## pitfalls
Assuming recapping a needle before disposal is a safe practice; two-handed recapping is a classic cause of needlestick injury and is specifically discouraged, along with breaking/bending the needle or manually separating it from the syringe.

## article_ids
ART-INF-OCCUPATIONAL-EXPOSURE-SHARPS-SAFETY

direct_statement

47- Proper disposal of used needles include which of the following? a. Always breaking or bending the needle before disposal b. Carefully removing needle from syringe c. Carefully recapping needle d. Placing needle & attached syringe in puncture resistant container [printed key: D]

# Item
## label
Clean latex gloves are the appropriate PPE for starting IV lines or phlebotomy

## id
CON-INF-606F4E2565AC67

## canonical_key
infection-control.ppe.selection-by-procedure

## definition
For routine procedures involving expected contact with blood, such as starting IV lines or phlebotomy, clean (non-sterile) latex gloves are the appropriate PPE -- a lower-barrier option than sterile gloves (reserved for procedures requiring a sterile field) but still providing blood/body-fluid barrier protection for the healthcare worker.

## explicit_objective
State that clean (non-sterile) latex gloves are the appropriate PPE for IV line placement and phlebotomy.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id

Mycology, virology and infection control

## subtopic
Occupational exposure and sharps safety

## microtopic
PPE selection by procedure

## nanotopic

## aliases
PPE for phlebotomy
Clean vs sterile gloves

## pitfalls
Assuming every blood-contact procedure requires sterile gloves or a sterile gown; routine venipuncture/IV access needs only clean (non-sterile) gloves for barrier protection, reserving sterile technique for procedures that must maintain a sterile field.

## article_ids
ART-INF-OCCUPATIONAL-EXPOSURE-SHARPS-SAFETY

direct_statement

# Item
## label
Hospital-acquired infection is one acquired in a healthcare setting more than 48 hours post-admission, not incubating before admission

## id
CON-INF-B18A3C1EEE39BE

## canonical_key
infection-control.definitions.hospital-acquired-infection

## definition
The term "hospital-acquired infection" (nosocomial infection) is best defined as an infection acquired in a healthcare setting more than 48 hours after admission, and which was not already incubating (present but not yet symptomatic) at the time of admission -- distinguishing it from a community-acquired infection or an infection that was already incubating before the patient arrived at the healthcare facility.

## explicit_objective
State the definition of hospital-acquired infection: acquired in a healthcare setting more than 48 hours post-admission, not incubating before admission.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## subject
inf

## primary_node_id
DIS-MIC-T01

## secondary_node_ids
SYS-FND-T05-S01
DIS-MIC

ASU-INF

## universities
asu

## learner_years
1

## blueprint_weight
0.25

## exam_weight_by_year
ASU_Y1=0.25

## clinical_relevance
0.3

## academic_relevance
0.85

## confidence
0.82

## topic
Mycology, virology and infection control

## subtopic
Aseptic technique, endoscope reprocessing and HAI definitions

## microtopic
Hospital-acquired infection definition

## nanotopic

## aliases
Nosocomial infection definition
Hospital-acquired infection (HAI)

## pitfalls
Using the wrong time threshold (24 hours instead of the correct 48 hours) or omitting the "not incubating before admission" clause, which is essential to exclude infections a patient already had brewing before they arrived at the facility.

## article_ids
ART-INF-ASEPTIC-TECHNIQUE-ENDOSCOPE-HAI-DEFINITIONS

## support_mode
direct_statement

## original_wording
49- Which of the following best describes the term "hospital acquired infection"? A) An infection acquired in the community B) An infection acquired in a healthcare setting >48 hours post admission and was not incubating before C) An infection acquired in a healthcare setting >24 hours post admission and was not incubating before D) An infection acquired in a healthcare setting 48 hours post admission and was incubating before [printed key: B]
